\begin{document}





\title[Approximate counting for mixed $p$-spin glasses]{Approximate Counting for Mixed $p$-Spin Glasses in Weak External Fields via Cluster Expansion}
\author[Wu]{Qiang Wu}
\address{Department of Mathematics, 1291 University of Oregon, 175 Prince Lucien Campbell Hall, Eugene, OR 97403-1205, USA}
\email{qiangwu@uoregon.edu}
\date{\today}
\subjclass[2020]{Primary: 82B44, 68W25; Secondary: 68Q87, 68W20.}
\keywords{Mixed $p$-spin glass, partition function, approximate counting, weak external fields, cluster expansion}
\thanks{}
%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\begin{abstract}
We give algorithms for approximating the partition function~$Z_N$ of the Sherrington--Kirkpatrick (SK) model and finite mixed Ising~$p$-spin models with $p\ge 3$. The algorithms apply at a fixed margin below the second-moment threshold~$\beta_{\mathrm f}$ with homogeneous weak external fields~$|h|\leq\rho_0N^{-1/4}$, for fixed~$\rho_0>0$. For any accuracy~$\varepsilon\in(0,1)$ and failure parameter~$\delta\in(0,1)$, they approximate~$\log Z_N$ to additive error~$\varepsilon$. The number of exact operations is polynomial jointly in~$N$, $\varepsilon^{-1}$, and~$\delta^{-1}$. The disorder variables are independent with a common symmetric law of unit variance. The failure probability is at most~$\delta$ plus an explicit disorder tail bound. This additional term tends to zero under a finite fourth moment condition on disorder. Our approach builds on the cluster expansions of Aizenman--Lebowitz--Ruelle~\cite{ALR87} and Dey--Wu~\cites{DW23,DW26}. For higher-order mixtures, we retain hypergraphs using few vertices and evaluate their total weight deterministically through local spin averages and inclusion--exclusion. For SK, we impose cutoffs on the number of vertices of degree at least three and on the total number of edges. We estimate the retained sum using randomized color coding~\cite{AYZ95}. A lower tail estimate based on reverse hypercontractivity~\cite{MOS13} converts the mean-square approximation of the normalized partition function into logarithmic accuracy.
\end{abstract}


%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%%
\maketitle
\setcounter{tocdepth}{1}\tableofcontents


\section{Introduction and main results}\label{sec:introduction}

Spin glasses are models in statistical physics described by Gibbs measures with random spin interactions. Determining their limiting free energy has been a central problem for decades. Talagrand~\cite{Tal06} and Panchenko~\cite{panchenko2014parisi} proved the Parisi formula for the Sherrington--Kirkpatrick (SK) model and its $p$-spin variants, giving a variational characterization of this limit. The algorithmic counterpart is instead asking how to compute the partition function of a finite system in polynomial time approximately or exactly. For the classical Ising model, this problem has been understood well, see a non-exhaustive list in~\cites{JS93,LLY13,PR17,LSS19}. For spin glasses, this algorithmic problem has only been studied recently. Gamarnik and K{\i}z{\i}lda{\u{g}}~\cite{GK21} established average case hardness results for ``exact" evaluation of the SK partition function. Several recent works present various (quasi)-polynomial time approximation algorithms in high temperature regimes~\cites{BHL25,HLMM26,SZ26,HLY26}. Here we develop a cluster expansion based approach to this problem, we defer discussions on its relation to other works in Section~\ref{subsec:related-work} and~\ref{subsec:concurrent-work}.


\subsection{Definitions}\label{subsec:model}

Let~$\mathcal P$ be a finite, nonempty set of interaction orders, with coefficients~\mbox{$a_p>0$} for~$p\in\mathcal P$. An interaction of order~$p$ involves~$p$ distinct spins. We consider the SK model, for which~$\mathcal P=\{2\}$ and~$a_2=1$, and higher-order mixtures, for which~$\mathcal P\subseteq\{3,4,\ldots\}$. Write~\mbox{$p_m=\min\mathcal P$} and~\mbox{$P=\max\mathcal P$}.

For~$N\geq P$, let~$[N]=\{1,\ldots,N\}$ and~$\Sigma_N=\{-1,1\}^N$ be the spin configuration space. The interactions of order~$p$ are the~$p$-element subsets~$\mathcal E_{N,p}=\binom{[N]}p$. Set
\begin{equation*}
\mathcal E_N=\bigcup_{p\in\mathcal P}\mathcal E_{N,p},\qquad
\sigma_e=\prod_{i\in e}\sigma_i\quad(\sigma\in\Sigma_N,\ e\in\mathcal E_N).
\end{equation*}
The disorder array~$J=(J_e)_{e\in\mathcal E_N}$ has independent entries with common law~$\mu$. Write~$\E$ and~$\pr$ for expectation and probability over the disorder. For~$J_0$ with law~$\mu$, assume
\begin{equation}\label{eq:disorder}
J_0\overset{\mathrm d}=-J_0,\qquad \E J_0^2=1\,.
\end{equation}
Namely the disorder is symmetric about zero and has unit variance.

For inverse temperature~$\beta\geq0$ and a homogeneous external field~$h\in\mathbb R$, define the Hamiltonian and partition function
\begin{align}
H_N(\sigma;J)&=\sum_{p\in\mathcal P}\frac{a_p}{N^{(p-1)/2}}\sum_{e\in\mathcal E_{N,p}}J_e\sigma_e,\label{eq:hamiltonian}\\
Z_N(\beta,h;J)&=\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma_N}\exp\!\left\{\beta H_N(\sigma;J)+h\sum_{i=1}^N\sigma_i\right\}\,.\label{eq:partition}
\end{align}
These interaction conventions are consistent with~\cite{DW23} for SK and~\cite{DW26}*{(1)--(2)} for higher-order mixtures. Following~\cite{DW26}*{(6)}, define the entropy function
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sign-entropy}
I(x)=\frac{1+x}{2}\log(1+x)+\frac{1-x}{2}\log(1-x),\qquad -1\leq x\leq1\,,
\end{equation}
 and the interaction polynomial and second-moment threshold
\begin{equation}\label{eq:graphical-threshold}
\xi(x)=\sum_{p\in\mathcal P}\frac{a_p^2x^p}{p!},\qquad
\beta_{\mathrm f}^{\,2}=\inf_{0<x\leq1}\frac{I(x)}{\xi(x)}\,.
\end{equation}
For~$0<x\leq1$, we have~$\xi(x)\leq\xi(1)x^2$ and~$I(x)\geq x^2/2$. The latter follows from~$I(0)=I'(0)=0$ and~$I''(x)=1/(1-x^2)\geq1$. Evaluating the ratio also at~$x=1$ gives
\begin{equation*}
0<\frac{1}{2\xi(1)}\leq\beta_{\mathrm f}^{\,2}\leq\frac{\log2}{\xi(1)}<\infty.
\end{equation*}
For~$\beta<\beta_{\mathrm f}$, we have~$\beta^2\xi(x)<I(x)$ for every~$x\in(0,1]$. This entropy comparison yields the second moment estimates in Section~\ref{sec:cluster-expansion}. For SK, $\xi(x)=x^2/2$ and~$I(x)/(x^2/2)\to1$ as~$x\downarrow0$, it gives~$\beta_{\mathrm f}=1$.

Fix~$0<\beta_0<\beta_{\mathrm f}$ and~$\rho_0>0$. We work in the following regime
\begin{equation}\label{eq:field-range}
0\leq\beta\leq\beta_0,\qquad |h|\leq\rho_0N^{-1/4}\,.
\end{equation}
The external field may depend on~$N$, and~$h=0$ is included. Constants may depend on the fixed parameters~$\mathcal P,(a_p),\beta_0,\rho_0$, but not on~$N$ or the particular~$\beta,h$ in this range.

\subsection{Approximate counting problem}\label{subsec:problem}

The algorithmic input consists of~$N$, a disorder realization~$J$, the parameters~$\beta,h$, a relative accuracy~$\varepsilon\in(0,1)$, and a failure allowance~$\delta\in(0,1)$. The goal is to return~$\widetilde Z_N$ satisfying
\begin{equation}\label{eq:relative-objective}
\left|\frac{\widetilde Z_N}{Z_N(\beta,h;J)}-1\right|\leq\varepsilon
\end{equation}
with a failure probability bound of~$\delta$ plus the disorder-dependent term in the main theorem. Accuracy is assessed over the disorder and, for SK, independent algorithmic randomness. We include this additional randomness in~$\E$ and~$\pr$ whenever an algorithmic output is involved. 

It suffices to approximate~$\log Z_N$ with $\widehat L_N$ in additive error at most~$\eta=\log(1+\varepsilon)$, then~$\widetilde Z_N=\exp(\widehat L_N)$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:log-to-relative}
\frac{1}{1+\varepsilon}\leq\frac{\widetilde Z_N}{Z_N}\leq1+\varepsilon\,,
\end{equation}
and hence~\eqref{eq:relative-objective}. This requires finer control than an~$o(1)$ approximation to the free energy~$N^{-1}\log Z_N$, which determines~$Z_N$ only up to a factor~$e^{o(N)}$.

Here the computational cost is measured by counting the number of exact operations, such as real arithmetic and evaluations of elementary functions. Our bounds do not account for bit complexity or the numerical precision needed to control roundoff errors.

\subsection{Main result}\label{subsec:main-results}

The following main result gives the guarantee for the algorithm~\textsc{LogApprox}, stated in Section~\ref{subsec:algorithms}. 

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:finite-size}
There are constants~$C,c>0$, depending only on~$\mathcal P,(a_p),\beta_0,\rho_0$, such that Algorithm~\ref{alg:log-approx}, for every~$N\geq P$, specified~$(\beta,h)$ satisfying~\eqref{eq:field-range}, and~$\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1)$, returns~$\widehat L_N$ in at most
\begin{equation}\label{eq:finite-size-cost}
CN^C\varepsilon^{-C}\delta^{-C}
\end{equation}
operations for every finite real input~$J$ and every realization of the internal randomness. For~$J_0$ with the common disorder law~$\mu$, set
\begin{align}
\tau_N=\sum_{p\in\mathcal P}\binom Np\pr\bigl(|a_pJ_0|>cN^{(p-1)/2}\bigr)\,.\label{eq:disorder-tail}
\end{align}
Under~\eqref{eq:disorder}, the error satisfies
\begin{align}
\pr\bigl(|\widehat L_N-\log Z_N(\beta,h;J)|>\varepsilon\bigr)&\leq\delta+\tau_N.\label{eq:finite-size-error}
\end{align}
For mixtures with~$p_m\geq3$, the algorithm is deterministic and probability is over~$J$. For SK, the algorithm is randomized and probability also includes its independent internal randomness. The constants and the algorithm are uniform over all disorder laws satisfying~\eqref{eq:disorder} and all specified parameters in these ranges.
\end{theorem}

 The term $\tau_N$ is due to an union bound to control the probability of some~$|a_pJ_e|$ exceeding~$cN^{(p-1)/2}$.  For any fixed parameter values within the stated ranges, the failure probability is at most~$\delta+\tau_N$. The event of success may depend on that choice, so we do not claim simultaneous accuracy over the entire parameter range. The cost exponent grows as~$\beta_0$ approaches~$\beta_{\mathrm f}$.

Note that a second moment condition on disorder alone need not make the bound on the tail probability small. The high probability consequences require higher moments control, such as  a finite fourth moment in the following Remark~\ref{rem:moment-consequences}.


\begin{remark}[Consequences under further moment assumptions]\label{rem:moment-consequences}\label{thm:relative-counting}\label{cor:polynomial-confidence}
Under~\eqref{eq:disorder}, bounds on~$\tau_N$ give the following consequences.
\begin{enumeratei}
\item If~$\E|J_0|^4<\infty$, then~$t^4\pr(|J_0|>t)\to0$ as~$t\to\infty$. Substituting the thresholds in~\eqref{eq:disorder-tail} gives~$\tau_N=\sum_{p\in\mathcal P}o(N^{2-p})=o(1)$, including SK. For relative tolerance~$\varepsilon\in(0,1)$, apply the theorem with logarithmic tolerance~$\eta=\log(1+\varepsilon)$ and~$\delta=1/(2N)$. Then~$\widetilde Z_N=\exp(\widehat L_N)>0$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:relative-counting-result}
\pr\left(\frac{\widetilde Z_N}{Z_N(\beta,h;J)}\in[1-\varepsilon,1+\varepsilon]\right)
\geq1-\tau_N-\frac1{2N}=1-o_N(1)\,.
\end{equation}
Since~$\eta\geq\varepsilon/2$, the cost is at most~$CN^C\varepsilon^{-C}$ after enlarging~$C$. For each fixed law, convergence is uniform over the specified~$\beta,h,\varepsilon$, though its rate may depend on the law.
\item Fix~$b>0$. If~$\E|J_0|^m<\infty$ for some~$m\geq2(p_m+b)/(p_m-1)$, then
\begin{equation*}
t^m\pr(|J_0|>t)\leq\E\bigl[|J_0|^m\ind_{\{|J_0|>t\}}\bigr]\longrightarrow0
\qquad(t\longrightarrow\infty).
\end{equation*}
Substituting the thresholds in~\eqref{eq:disorder-tail} gives~$\tau_N=\sum_{p\in\mathcal P}o(N^{p-m(p-1)/2})=o(N^{-b})$, since~$m>2$ and every exponent is at most~$-b$. Taking~$\delta=N^{-b}/2$ gives logarithmic error at most~$\varepsilon$ with failure probability at most~$N^{-b}$ for all sufficiently large~$N$, at cost~$C_bN^{C_b}\varepsilon^{-C_b}$. The constant~$C_b$ depends only on the fixed model, $\beta_0,\rho_0,b$. The required lower bound on~$N$ may also depend on the law, but is uniform over the specified~$\beta,h,\varepsilon$.
\end{enumeratei}
Both conclusions retain the determinism and probability conventions of the theorem. For bounded symmetric disorder, $\tau_N=0$ for all sufficiently large~$N$.
\end{remark}


\subsection{The algorithm}\label{subsec:algorithms}

We first separate an explicitly computable factor from the partition function $Z_N$, which is basically the first step of cluster expansion in~\cite{ALR87,DW23,DW26}. For each interaction~$e$, let
\begin{equation*}
x_e=\frac{\beta a_{|e|}J_e}{N^{(|e|-1)/2}}.
\end{equation*}
Define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:algorithm-normalization}
\overline Z_N=\prod_{e\in\mathcal E_N}\cosh x_e,
\qquad \widehat Z_N=\frac{Z_N}{(2\cosh h)^N\,\overline Z_N}\,.
\end{equation}
Here~$\widehat Z_N$ is a normalized partition function. The algorithm thus computes~$N\log(2\cosh h)+\log\overline Z_N$ exactly and approximates~$\log\widehat Z_N$.

For a target mean-square error~$u\in(0,1)$ in approximating the normalized partition function~$\widehat Z_N$, the subroutine~\textsc{GraphApprox} (Algorithm~\ref{alg:graph-approx}) returns an approximation~$T$ satisfying
\(
\E|T-\widehat Z_N|^2\leq u
\)
in~$CN^Cu^{-C}$ operations (Proposition~\ref{prop:mean-square}). To obtain a logarithmic approximation, we also need to know that~$\widehat Z_N$ is unlikely to be very small. Proposition~\ref{prop:lower-tail} provides fixed constants~$C_+\geq1$ and~$s>0$ such that, for every level~$z>0$,
\begin{equation*}
\pr(\widehat Z_N<z)\leq C_+z^s+\tau_N.
\end{equation*}
Their explicit values are given in~\eqref{eq:algorithm-mass-constant} and~\eqref{eq:lower-tail-constants}.

\begin{algorithm}[!htbp]
\caption{\textsc{LogApprox}$(N,\beta,h,J,\varepsilon,\delta)$}\label{alg:log-approx}
\begin{algorithmic}[1]
\Require Disorder~$J$ with finite real entries, $N\geq P$, parameters~$(\beta,h)$ as in~\eqref{eq:field-range}, additive logarithmic tolerance~$\varepsilon\in(0,1)$, and failure allowance~$\delta\in(0,1)$.
\State $L_{\mathrm{base}}\gets N\log(2\cosh h)+\sum_{e\in\mathcal E_N}\log\cosh x_e$.
\State $z\gets(\delta/(2C_+))^{1/s}$ and~$u\gets\delta\varepsilon^2z^2/8$.
\State $T\gets\textsc{GraphApprox}(N,\beta,h,J,u)$.
\If{$T>0$}
\State \Return $\widehat L_N=L_{\mathrm{base}}+\log T$.
\Else
\State \Return $\widehat L_N=L_{\mathrm{base}}$.
\EndIf
\end{algorithmic}
\end{algorithm}


For these choices of $z$ and $u$, the lower-tail bound and Markov’s inequality imply that $\widehat Z_N\geq z$ and $|T-\widehat Z_N|\leq\varepsilon z/2$ hold together with probability at least $1-\delta-\tau_N$. The error is then small relative to $\widehat Z_N$, giving $T>0$ and $|\log T-\log\widehat Z_N|\leq\varepsilon$. Since $u^{-1}$ is bounded by a polynomial in $\varepsilon^{-1}$ and $\delta^{-1}$, the operation count is polynomial jointly in $N,\varepsilon^{-1},\delta^{-1}$.


%The level~$z$ makes the probability of a small value at most~$\delta/2+\tau_N$. The budget~$u$ makes the probability of~$|T-\widehat Z_N|>\varepsilon z/2$ at most~$\delta/2$, by Markov's inequality. Outside these two events, $T>0$ and the logarithmic error is at most~$\varepsilon$. Since~$u^{-1}$ is a product of fixed powers of~$\varepsilon^{-1}$ and~$\delta^{-1}$ up to a fixed factor, the total cost is polynomial jointly in~$N,\varepsilon^{-1},\delta^{-1}$. Section~\ref{subsec:main-proofs} gives the error calculation. The next subsection explains the construction of~\textsc{GraphApprox} and the lower-tail estimate.

\subsection{Ideas of the proof}\label{subsec:proof-sketch} The proof for Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-size} contains three major steps. The first step is using the cluster expansion in~\cite{ALR87,DW23,DW26} to get accurate estimate for the variance order of large graph clusters, which determines what graphs to be discarded. The second step is an efficient algorithmic procedure to sum the retained graphs. The last step is about controlling the lower tail probability of $\hat{Z}_N$, which translates the approximation error into logarithm accuracy.

\paragraph{Expansion and error estimate.} Let~$\omega_e=\tanh x_e$ and~$\hh=\tanh h$. For a graph~$\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal E_N$, write the degree of vertex $i$ in $\Gamma$ as~$\deg_\Gamma(i)=|\{e\in\Gamma:i\in e\}|$ and let~$\partial\Gamma=\{i\in[N]:\deg_\Gamma(i)\text{ is odd}\}$ be the odd degree vertices. Expanding the interaction factors and averaging the spins gives
\begin{equation*}
\widehat Z_N=\sum_{\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal E_N}W_h(\Gamma),
\qquad W_h(\Gamma)=\hh^{|\partial\Gamma|}\prod_{e\in\Gamma}\omega_e.
\end{equation*}
Independence and symmetry of the disorder make the cross terms vanish when we square the error and take expectation. Thus, for a fixed family~$\mathcal R$ of graphs,
\begin{equation*}
\E\left|\widehat Z_N-\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathcal R}W_h(\Gamma)\right|^2
=\sum_{\Gamma\notin\mathcal R}\E W_h(\Gamma)^2.
\end{equation*}
We choose which graphs to keep so that the sum on the right is small. We then need to compute or estimate their total weight in polynomial time.

\paragraph{Mixed $p$-spin analysis} For an interaction of order~$p\ge 3$, $\E\omega_e^2=O(N^{-(p-1)})$. Lemma~\ref{lem:higher-support-mass} bounds the sum of second moments over all hypergraphs using~$v$ vertices by~$(Cv/N)^{\kappa v}$ for fixed~$C,\kappa>0$. This bound decreases rapidly when~$v$ is a small fraction of~$N$. We keep all hypergraphs using at most~$k$ vertices. For a fixed set~$S$ with~$|S|\leq k$, let~$G(S)$ be the average of~$\prod_{e\in\mathcal E_N,\,e\subseteq S}(1+\omega_e\sigma_e)$ over independent spins on~$S$ with mean~$\hh$. It sums the weights of all hypergraphs contained in~$S$. A hypergraph may occur in several such averages. Inclusion--exclusion gives coefficients depending only on~$|S|$ that count each retained hypergraph once. For~$k<N$, each~$G(S)$ is evaluated once, using~$2^{|S|}$ spin configurations, for a total of~$\sum_{j=0}^{k}\binom Nj2^j$ spin configurations, with polynomial work per configuration. 

\paragraph{Analysis for SK} In the SK case, for fixed cycle length~$\ell$, the number of cycles is of order~$N^\ell$, and each has second moment of order~$N^{-\ell}$. The sum of their second moments therefore has constant order. We instead count by \emph{branching vertices}, i.e. those of degree at least three. For~$b\geq1$, Lemma~\ref{lem:sk-branching-mass} bounds the sum of second moments for graphs with~$b$ branching vertices by~$C(Cb/N)^b$. We keep at most~$k$ branching vertices and~$L$ edges to exclude long paths and cycles. Fix the branching set and add an auxiliary vertex and join it to each odd-degree vertex by an edge of weight~$\hh$ to account for the external field factors. Every used vertex outside this enlarged set now has degree two. Apart from edges within the enlarged set, the resulting graph consists of paths and cycles whose outside vertices do not overlap. Color coding~\cite{AYZ95} provides an efficient way to compute the sum of those graph structures. The cost per branching set and coloring is polynomial in~$N$ and exponential in~$k+L$ (Section~\ref{sec:sk}).

\paragraph{Completing the proof.} We first choose the cutoffs to obtain a mean-square error at most~$u$ at a cost polynomial in~$N$ and~$u^{-1}$. Here~$k$ counts all used vertices for higher-order mixtures and branching vertices for SK. For cutoffs~$k$ below a small fixed fraction of~$N$, the mean-square truncation error bounds decrease exponentially in~$k\log(N/k)$, while the number of vertex sets of size~$k$ is at most~$(eN/k)^k$. Balancing these two bounds gives the required cutoffs. For SK, the mean-square contribution from graphs with more than~$L$ edges decreases exponentially in~$L$. Choosing~$L$ large enough to make this contribution at most a fixed fraction of~$u$ requires only~$L=O(1+\log(1/u))$. The mean-square evaluation error is bounded by a factor exponential in~$L$ divided by the number of independent colorings used for each candidate branching set. Thus a number of colorings polynomial in~$u^{-1}$ suffices to make this error at most a fixed fraction of~$u$ as well.

To turn this absolute approximation into logarithmic accuracy, we must ensure that~$\widehat Z_N$ is unlikely to be very small. We obtain the required lower-tail bound from reverse hypercontractivity~\cite{MOS13}, using the symmetry of the disorder and the fixed gap between~$\beta_0$ and~$\beta_{\mathrm f}$. Combining this bound with the mean-square approximation gives the desired logarithmic accuracy with failure probability at most~$\delta+\tau_N$, while preserving polynomial cost.

\subsection{Related work}\label{subsec:related-work}

%Our starting point is the cluster expansion of Aizenman--Lebowitz--Ruelle~\cite{ALR87} for SK at zero field. After removing the product of hyperbolic cosines, they use disorder symmetry and independence to obtain orthogonal graph weights and control large graphs. Dey--Wu later extend this approach to SK in weak external fields~\cite{DW23}, where paths contribute alongside cycles, and to distinct-index mixed~$p$-spin models~\cite{DW26}. These fluctuation results provide the second-moment estimates on which we build. For approximate counting, one must additionally choose truncations for a requested accuracy and evaluate the retained signed sums efficiently. Section~\ref{subsec:concurrent-work} compares how the present and concurrent works address these tasks.


Barvinok's interpolation method approximates the logarithm of the partition function by computing a truncated Taylor series. Bounds on the locations of the complex zeros control the truncation error, providing the basis for the zero-freeness approach to approximate counting. For applications to classical spin systems and related counting problems, see Barvinok~\cite{Bar17}, Barvinok and Barvinok~\cite{BB21}, Patel and Regts~\cite{PR17}, and Liu, Sinclair and Srivastava~\cite{LSS19}. In the spin glass setting, Bencs, Huang, Lee, Liu and Regts~\cite{BHL25} obtain deterministic algorithms with quasipolynomial running time for finite Gaussian mixed Ising models at zero external field, with high probability over the disorder at a fixed margin below the second-moment threshold. Their models include mixtures of pair and higher-order interactions, indexed by ordered tuples with repeated sites allowed. After matching interaction normalizations, their Ising threshold agrees with~$\beta_{\mathrm f}$. Our SK algorithm is randomized, with an operation bound polynomial jointly in system size, inverse requested accuracy, and inverse failure allowance.

Sampling also provides a route to approximate counting by estimating successive ratios of partition functions along a temperature schedule. For SK, functional inequalities yield efficient sampling at sufficiently high temperature, as shown by Eldan, Koehler, and Zeitouni~\cite{EKZ22} and Anari, Koehler, and Vuong~\cite{AKV24}. For Gaussian SK at zero external field, the stochastic localization approach of El Alaoui, Montanari, and Sellke, together with the work of Celentano, gives efficient sampling in normalized Wasserstein distance throughout~$\beta<1$~\cites{AMS25,Cel24}. Lee, Sandhu, and Shi obtain vanishing total variation error in the same temperature range~\cite{LSS26}.

To obtain accurate counts by annealing, one must control the sampling error at every temperature used in the schedule. Hou, Liu, and Yu~\cite{HLY26}*{Theorem~1.2 and Corollary~1.3} provide such control through a weak Poincar\'e inequality and simulated annealing with Glauber dynamics for Gaussian SK at zero external field throughout~$\beta<1$. Their counting algorithm achieves prescribed inverse polynomial relative accuracy and failure probabilities in polynomial time for sufficiently large~$N$. The polynomial exponent may depend on the prescribed accuracy and confidence powers.

The spectral-gap argument in the OpenAI mathematics release~\cite{OAI26gap} provides another sampling-based route for Gaussian SK at zero external field. As observed by Harbuzova--Lepsveridze--Majid--Moitra~\cite{HLMM26}*{Section~1.2}, making the estimates uniform on fixed subcritical temperature intervals and applying annealing yields randomized polynomial-time relative approximation at every fixed~$\beta<1$, with high probability over the disorder. These approaches obtain counts from Gibbs samples, whereas our algorithm directly sums retained graph weights. Conversely, the counting-to-sampling reduction of Jerrum--Valiant--Vazirani~\cite{JVV86} requires counts for models obtained by fixing spins. Such conditioning can introduce site-dependent external fields and lower-order interactions outside our model class, so our theorem does not directly provide the required counting oracle.

For related algorithmic results in other disordered systems, see~\cites{HLP23,AEGP26,Han26} for the random field Ising model and~\cites{Ho25,LW25} for the continuous random energy model.

%Our SK summation uses the color-coding method of Alon--Yuster--Zwick~\cite{AYZ95} and dynamic programming on color sets. Combining pieces with disjoint color sets is also related to the subset-convolution methods of Bj\"orklund--Husfeldt--Kaski--Koivisto~\cite{BHKK07}. The concurrent counting algorithms most closely related to our construction are discussed in the next subsection.

\subsection{Concurrent work}\label{subsec:concurrent-work}

Harbuzova et~al.~\cite{HLMM26}*{Theorem~1.1}\footnote{A preliminary draft of this work and a companion Lean formalization were posted on \href{https://github.com/qiangwu2/AI-assisted-results/tree/main/approximate-counting-spin-glasses}{GitHub} on October~7, after OpenAI's mathematics release. A few hours later, the paper~\cite{HLMM26} appeared in arXiv's daily announcement.} and Schmidhuber and Zlokapa~\cite{SZ26}*{Theorems~26 and~31} obtain deterministic algorithms for approximate counting in Gaussian SK at zero external field throughout~$\beta<1$, with polynomial running time and high probability of success over the disorder. Our SK algorithm is randomized and allows symmetric disorder and homogeneous weak external fields, subject to our explicit disorder tail bound. For mixtures, \cite{HLMM26} also covers models containing both pair interactions and interactions of higher order, which our results exclude. After matching interaction normalizations, their threshold agrees with~$\beta_{\mathrm f}$, although their model at finite~$N$ includes interactions with repeated sites. For mixtures whose orders are all at least three, \cite{SZ26} reaches the static transition, which can lie above~$\beta_{\mathrm f}$.

The complexity guarantees also differ. Like~\cite{HLMM26}, we obtain a bound on the number of exact operations that is polynomial jointly in system size, inverse requested accuracy, and inverse failure allowance. Our failure bound includes the disorder tail term~$\tau_N$, while theirs includes a vanishing term from a zero-freeness estimate. The guarantees in~\cite{SZ26} allow accuracy and failure targets given by any fixed inverse powers of~$N$, with polynomial running time whose exponent may depend on those powers. They also establish polynomial bit complexity, whereas our operation bounds do not account for numerical precision.

For SK at zero external field, all three methods separate branching vertices from paths and cycles through vertices of degree two and use color coding to sum these pieces. The construction is particularly close to~\cite{HLMM26}*{Section~5}, which also combines piece weights through polynomial multiplication. Both concurrent algorithms are deterministic. We average independent random colorings and introduce an auxiliary vertex to account for the weak external field. For mixtures whose orders are all at least three, our algorithm sums the retained hypergraphs exactly through local spin averages and inclusion--exclusion. The corresponding methods in~\cite{HLMM26}*{Appendix~A} and~\cite{SZ26}*{Section~7} use an extension of color coding and a Hermite expansion with cavity calculations, respectively. Our treatment of small partition function values also differs from~\cite{HLMM26}. We use reverse hypercontractivity for the coupling signs, rather than zero-freeness, to convert approximation in mean square into logarithmic accuracy.

\subsection{AI disclosure}\label{subsec:ai-disclosure}

This work was developed through extensive interactions with GPT-5.6 Pro and GPT-6 Pro. The author originally proposed using the cluster expansion to obtain approximate counting algorithms by discarding large graph clusters, motivated by the work of Dey--Wu~\cites{DW23,DW26}. In particular, the fast decay of the second moments of large hypergraph clusters in the higher-order models suggested that a relatively small retained sum might suffice. Starting from this idea, GPT-5.6 Pro proposed a polynomial time algorithm for the higher order case based on a cutoff on the number of hyperedges. However, the initial approach only gave a quasi-polynomial time algorithm for SK.

Further interactions with  GPT-6 Pro focused on improving the SK case. During the process, the models suggested using the established color-coding method~\cite{AYZ95} to sum the retained paths and cycles. The author had not previously been familiar with color coding and spent time studying the method and its application here. After several rounds of discussion and revision, the truncation was organized by the number of vertices for higher-order mixtures and by \emph{branching vertices} for SK, yielding a common framework for choosing the cutoffs enabling efficient algorithms.

Most of the proofs in Section~\ref{sec:sk}, particularly those concerning color coding and the evaluation of the retained sum, were developed through these interactions with AI. The author checked all proofs, reread and revised the manuscript many times, and takes responsibility for its mathematical claims, interpretation, and final presentation. 

\section{Cluster expansion and second moment estimates}\label{sec:cluster-expansion}

We establish the following two estimates shared by the algorithms. Orthogonality expresses the mean-square truncation error as a sum of expected squared graph weights. A bound at a inverse temperature below the threshold $\beta_f$ then controls this sum and gives exponential decay in the number of edges. Throughout Sections~\ref{sec:cluster-expansion}--\ref{sec:error-complexity}, the assumptions of Section~\ref{subsec:model} hold, in particular~$N\geq P$, $0\leq\beta\leq\beta_0<\beta_{\mathrm f}$, and~$|h|\leq\rho_0N^{-1/4}$.

\subsection{Expansion and orthogonality}\label{subsec:expansion}

Recall~$\omega_e=\tanh x_e$ and~$\hh=\tanh h$. An interaction set~$\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal E_N$ is a graph for SK and a hypergraph otherwise. Let~$V(\Gamma)=\bigcup_{e\in\Gamma}e$ be the vertex set of $\Gamma$ and $\partial\Gamma$ be its odd-degree vertices. Define the graph weights by
\begin{equation*}
\omega(\Gamma)=\prod_{e\in\Gamma}\omega_e,
\qquad W_h(\Gamma)=\hh^{|\partial\Gamma|}\omega(\Gamma).
\end{equation*}
Unless restrictions are stated, sums over~$\Gamma$ range over all subsets of~$\mathcal E_N$, including the empty graph. Empty products and zeroth powers equal one.

For any external field~$h\in\mathbb R$, let~$\pr_h$ be the law of independent spins with
\begin{equation*}
\pr_h(\sigma_i=\pm1)=\frac{e^{\pm h}}{2\cosh h},\qquad i\in[N],
\end{equation*}
and let~$\E_h$ be its expectation. In particular, $\E_h\sigma_i=\hh$.  Applying~$e^{x_e\sigma_e}=\cosh(x_e)(1+\omega_e\sigma_e)$ to each interaction in~$Z_N$, then using the normalization~\eqref{eq:algorithm-normalization}, gives~$\widehat Z_N=\E_h\prod_e(1+\omega_e\sigma_e)$. In the product expansion, selecting the interactions in~$\Gamma$ contributes~$\omega(\Gamma)\prod_{e\in\Gamma}\sigma_e$. Independence of the spins gives
\begin{equation*}
\E_h\prod_{e\in\Gamma}\sigma_e
=\prod_{i\in V(\Gamma)}\E_h\sigma_i^{\deg_\Gamma(i)}
=\hh^{|\partial\Gamma|}.
\end{equation*}
The last equality uses~$\sigma_i^2=1$ and~$\E_h\sigma_i=\hh$. Summing over~$\Gamma$ gives the expansion of~\cite{DW23} and~\cite{DW26}*{(3)--(5), (38), and~(40)}
\begin{equation}\label{eq:algorithm-expansion}
\widehat Z_N=\E_h\prod_{e\in\mathcal E_N}(1+\omega_e\sigma_e)
=\sum_{\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal E_N}W_h(\Gamma)>0\,.
\end{equation}
Positivity follows from~$|\omega_e|<1$. Symmetry gives~$\E\omega_e=0$. For~$e\in\mathcal E_{N,p}$, denote the common second moment~$\E\omega_e^2$ by~$r_{N,p}$. Since~$\tanh^2x\leq x^2$ and~$\E J_0^2=1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:edge-second-moment}
r_{N,p}:=\E\tanh^2\!\left(\frac{\beta a_pJ_0}{N^{(p-1)/2}}\right)
\leq\frac{\beta_0^2a_p^2}{N^{p-1}}\,.
\end{equation}
We allow random coefficients in the next identity because the SK evaluator uses colors drawn independently of the disorder.

\begin{lemma}[Orthogonality]\label{lem:orthogonality}
For~$\Gamma,\Gamma'\subseteq\mathcal E_N$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:orthogonality}
\E[\omega(\Gamma)\omega(\Gamma')]=\ind_{\{\Gamma=\Gamma'\}}\prod_{e\in\Gamma}r_{N,|e|}\,.
\end{equation}
Let~$(c_\Gamma)_{\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal E_N}$ be square-integrable real random coefficients whose full array is independent of~$J$. With expectation over both~$J$ and this array,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:random-orthogonality}
\E\left|\sum_\Gamma c_\Gamma W_h(\Gamma)\right|^2
=\sum_\Gamma\E[c_\Gamma^2]\hh^{2|\partial\Gamma|}\prod_{e\in\Gamma}r_{N,|e|}\,.
\end{equation}
The coefficients may depend on one another.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
If~$\Gamma\ne\Gamma'$, at least an edge in their symmetric difference appears once in the product. Its centered weight is independent of the other edge weights, so the expectation vanishes. If~$\Gamma=\Gamma'$, independence gives the second moments. Conditioning on the coefficient array and expanding the square proves~\eqref{eq:random-orthogonality}.
\end{proof}

In particular, for a deterministic family~$\mathcal R$ of retained graphs,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:truncation-isometry}
\E\left|\widehat Z_N-\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathcal R}W_h(\Gamma)\right|^2
=\sum_{\substack{\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal E_N\\\Gamma\notin\mathcal R}}
\hh^{2|\partial\Gamma|}\prod_{e\in\Gamma}r_{N,|e|}\,.
\end{equation}
Thus the error is determined by the expected squared weights of the discarded graphs. We next give the second moment estimates for those graphs.

\subsection{Second moments and the edge tail}\label{subsec:expansion-mass}

Choose a comparison inverse temperature strictly between~$\beta_0$ and~$\beta_{\mathrm f}$ by setting
\begin{equation}\label{eq:algorithm-temperature-constants}
\beta_+=\frac{\beta_0+\beta_{\mathrm f}}2,\qquad
\theta=\frac{\beta_0}{\beta_+}\in(0,1)\,.
\end{equation}
For~$p\in\mathcal P$, define the comparison weights by
\begin{equation*}
\bar r_{N,p}=\frac{\beta_+^2a_p^2}{N^{p-1}},
\qquad r_{N,p}\leq\theta^2\bar r_{N,p}.
\end{equation*}
Here~$\bar r_{N,p}$ is a deterministic upper bound, not the exact edge second moment at~$\beta_+$. Bounding the graph sum with these larger weights will leave a factor~$\theta^2$ for each original edge. Let
\begin{equation*}
\Delta=\sum_{p\in\mathcal P}\frac{a_p^2}{2(p-2)!},
\qquad \lambda_+=\frac{\beta_+^2}{\beta_{\mathrm f}^2}<1.
\end{equation*}
Define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:algorithm-mass-constant}
C_+=\exp\!\left\{\beta_+^2\Delta+\frac{1+\lambda_+}{2(1-\lambda_+)}\rho_0^4\right\}
\frac{3+\lambda_+}{1-\lambda_+}\,.
\end{equation}
The proof follows the second-moment arguments of~\cite{DW23}*{Lemma~4.1} and~\cite{DW26}*{Lemma~3.1 and Proposition~3.3}, with an explicit bound for every~$N\geq P$ under~\eqref{eq:disorder}.

\begin{proposition}[Second moments and edge tail]\label{prop:mass}
For every~$N\geq P$ and~$|h|\leq\rho_0N^{-1/4}$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:total-mass}
\sum_{\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal E_N}\hh^{2|\partial\Gamma|}
\prod_{e\in\Gamma}\bar r_{N,|e|}\leq C_+\,.
\end{equation}
For every real~$t\geq0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:edge-tail}
\sum_{\substack{\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal E_N\\|\Gamma|>t}}
\hh^{2|\partial\Gamma|}\prod_{e\in\Gamma}r_{N,|e|}
\leq C_+\theta^{2(\lfloor t\rfloor+1)}\leq C_+\theta^{2t}\,.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
To prove~\eqref{eq:total-mass}, we rewrite the graph sum as a spin average. Its external field factor is~$\hh^{2|\partial\Gamma|}$, so the new spins should have mean~$\hh^2$. Define the tilted external field by
\begin{equation*}
\widetilde h=\operatorname{arctanh}(\hh^2)=\frac12\log\cosh(2h).
\end{equation*}
Then~$\tanh\widetilde h=\hh^2$ and~$0\leq\widetilde h\leq h^2$, by~$\log\cosh y\leq y^2/2$. Applying the product expansion with external field~$\widetilde h$ and edge weights~$\bar r_{N,|e|}$ gives
\begin{equation*}
\sum_\Gamma\hh^{2|\partial\Gamma|}\prod_{e\in\Gamma}\bar r_{N,|e|}
=\E_{\widetilde h}\prod_{e\in\mathcal E_N}(1+\bar r_{N,|e|}\sigma_e).
\end{equation*}
To use the entropy comparison, we express the interaction sum in terms of~$S_N=\sum_{i=1}^N\sigma_i$. For~$p\in\mathcal P$, let~$R_{N,p}$ be the sum of terms in~$S_N^p$ indexed by ordered tuples with a repeated site. There are at most~$\binom p2N^{p-1}$ such tuples, each contributing a term of absolute value one. Thus
\begin{equation*}
S_N^p=p!\sum_{e\in\mathcal E_{N,p}}\sigma_e+R_{N,p},
\qquad |R_{N,p}|\leq\binom p2N^{p-1}.
\end{equation*}
For each~$p\in\mathcal P$, divide by~$p!$ and use~$\binom p2/p!=1/(2(p-2)!)$. Multiplying by~$\bar r_{N,p}=\beta_+^2a_p^2/N^{p-1}$ gives
\begin{equation*}
\bar r_{N,p}\sum_{e\in\mathcal E_{N,p}}\sigma_e
\leq \frac{\beta_+^2a_p^2}{p!}\,N\left(\frac{S_N}{N}\right)^p
+\frac{\beta_+^2a_p^2}{2(p-2)!}.
\end{equation*}
Summing over~$p$, the first terms give~$\beta_+^2N\xi(S_N/N)$ and the remainder terms give~$\beta_+^2\Delta$. Thus
\begin{equation*}
\sum_{e\in\mathcal E_N}\bar r_{N,|e|}\sigma_e
\leq\beta_+^2N\xi(S_N/N)+\beta_+^2\Delta.
\end{equation*}

All coefficients of~$\xi$ are nonnegative, so~$\xi(x)\leq\xi(|x|)$. The definition of~$\beta_{\mathrm f}$ and evenness of~$I$ therefore imply~$\beta_+^2\xi(x)\leq\lambda_+I(x)$ on~$[-1,1]$. To bound the product by the exponential of this sum, we also need its factors to be nonnegative. The inequality~$\beta_+^2\xi(1)<I(1)=\log2$ gives~$\beta_+^2a_p^2<p!\log2$. Since~$p!\leq p^{p-1}\leq N^{p-1}$, we have~$\bar r_{N,p}<1$. Define~$X_N=NI(S_N/N)$. For each spin configuration, all factors~$1+\bar r_{N,|e|}\sigma_e$ are positive. Applying~$1+x\leq e^x$ to each factor gives
\begin{equation*}
\prod_{e\in\mathcal E_N}(1+\bar r_{N,|e|}\sigma_e)
\leq\exp\!\left(\sum_{e\in\mathcal E_N}\bar r_{N,|e|}\sigma_e\right)
\leq e^{\beta_+^2\Delta+\lambda_+X_N}.
\end{equation*}
Taking~$\E_{\widetilde h}$ bounds the graph sum in~\eqref{eq:total-mass} by~$e^{\beta_+^2\Delta}\E_{\widetilde h}e^{\lambda_+X_N}$.

It remains to bound~$\E_{\widetilde h}e^{\lambda_+X_N}$ uniformly in~$N$. First change to the uniform spin measure~$\pr_0$. The probability of a configuration under~$\pr_{\widetilde h}$ is~$2^{-N}e^{\widetilde hS_N}/\cosh(\widetilde h)^N$, so
\begin{equation*}
\E_{\widetilde h}e^{\lambda_+X_N}
=\frac{\E_0\!\left[e^{\lambda_+X_N}e^{\widetilde hS_N}\right]}
{\cosh(\widetilde h)^N}.
\end{equation*}
We apply H\"older's inequality under~$\pr_0$ to the numerator. Set
\begin{equation*}
q_1=\frac{1+\lambda_+}{2\lambda_+},
\qquad q_2=\frac{1+\lambda_+}{1-\lambda_+},
\qquad v=q_1\lambda_+=\frac{1+\lambda_+}{2}<1.
\end{equation*}
Since~$q_1,q_2>1$ and~$1/q_1+1/q_2=1$, H\"older's inequality gives
\begin{equation*}
\E_0\!\left[e^{\lambda_+X_N}e^{\widetilde hS_N}\right]
\leq\bigl(\E_0e^{vX_N}\bigr)^{1/q_1}
\bigl(\E_0e^{q_2\widetilde hS_N}\bigr)^{1/q_2}.
\end{equation*}
Under~$\pr_0$, the spins are independent and uniform on~$\{-1,1\}$, so
\begin{equation*}
\E_0e^{q_2\widetilde hS_N}
=\prod_{i=1}^N\E_0e^{q_2\widetilde h\sigma_i}
=\left(\frac{e^{q_2\widetilde h}+e^{-q_2\widetilde h}}2\right)^N
=\cosh(q_2\widetilde h)^N.
\end{equation*}
Substituting this identity and dividing by~$\cosh(\widetilde h)^N$ yields
\begin{equation*}
\E_{\widetilde h}e^{\lambda_+X_N}
\leq\bigl(\E_0e^{vX_N}\bigr)^{1/q_1}
\frac{\cosh(q_2\widetilde h)^{N/q_2}}{\cosh(\widetilde h)^N}.
\end{equation*}
To bound~$\E_0e^{vX_N}$, we first estimate the tail probability of~$X_N$. Since~$I$ is even, $X_N=NI(|S_N|/N)$. Strict monotonicity of~$I$ on~$[0,1]$ gives, for~$0\leq x\leq1$,
\begin{align*}
\pr_0(X_N>NI(x))
=\pr_0(|S_N|>Nx)
\leq2\pr_0(S_N\geq Nx)
\leq2e^{-NI(x)}.
\end{align*}
The two inequalities use symmetry of~$S_N$ and the Chernoff bound, respectively. As~$x$ ranges over~$[0,1]$, $NI(x)$ varies continuously from~$0$ to~$N\log2$. Writing~$t=NI(x)$ therefore gives~$\pr_0(X_N>t)\leq2e^{-t}$ for~$0\leq t\leq N\log2$. For larger~$t$, the probability is zero because~$X_N\leq N\log2$. Since~$X_N\geq0$, integrating this tail bound yields
\begin{align*}
\E_0e^{vX_N}
&=1+v\int_0^\infty e^{vt}\pr_0(X_N>t)\,dt\\
&\leq1+2v\int_0^\infty e^{-(1-v)t}\,dt\\
&=1+\frac{2v}{1-v}=\frac{3+\lambda_+}{1-\lambda_+}.
\end{align*}
The integral of~$e^{-(1-v)t}$ is finite because~$v<1$. Since~$q_1>1$ and~$(3+\lambda_+)/(1-\lambda_+)>1$, the first factor satisfies
\begin{equation*}
\bigl(\E_0e^{vX_N}\bigr)^{1/q_1}
\leq\left(\frac{3+\lambda_+}{1-\lambda_+}\right)^{1/q_1}
\leq\frac{3+\lambda_+}{1-\lambda_+}.
\end{equation*}
For the second factor, use~$\cosh\widetilde h\geq1$ to discard its denominator. The bound~$\log\cosh y\leq y^2/2$ then gives~$\cosh(q_2\widetilde h)^{N/q_2}\leq e^{Nq_2\widetilde h^{\,2}/2}\leq e^{q_2\rho_0^4/2}$, where the last step uses~$\widetilde h\leq h^2$ and~$Nh^4\leq\rho_0^4$. These estimates prove~\eqref{eq:total-mass}.

To prove the bound~\eqref{eq:edge-tail}, apply~$r_{N,p}\leq\theta^2\bar r_{N,p}$ to every edge. For a graph with more than~$t$ edges, the product of these factors is at most~$\theta^{2(\lfloor t\rfloor+1)}$. Summing over these graphs and using~\eqref{eq:total-mass} gives the result.
\end{proof}

In particular, using the above results we have
\(
\E\widehat Z_N^2=\sum_\Gamma\E W_h(\Gamma)^2\leq C_+.
\)
The estimate~\eqref{eq:total-mass} will also be used in the lower tail control of $\hat{Z}_N$. For any~$u\in(0,1)$, \eqref{eq:truncation-isometry} and~\eqref{eq:edge-tail} show that we can approximate~$\widehat Z_N$ with mean-square error at most~$u$ by retaining all graphs with at most~$L$ edges, for a suitable choice of~$L=O(1+\log(1/u))$. Directly listing the retained graphs gives a cost bound~$N^{O(L)}$, which does not establish polynomial dependence jointly on~$N,u^{-1}$. The next two sections choose graph families that can be summed more efficiently.

\section{Mixed $p$-spin models with $p\ge 3$}\label{sec:pure}\label{sec:algorithm}

We truncate by the number of used vertices, without restricting the number of edges or components on a retained vertex set. Section~\ref{subsec:higher-support} defines the truncation and bounds the discarded part. Section~\ref{subsec:pure-evaluation} evaluates the retained sum by local spin averages. The main estimate must account for both the small second moments of the edges and the number of ways to choose the vertex labels. For example, in the pure three-spin model, a fixed hypergraph shape with~$v$ vertices, all of degree two, has~$2v/3$ edges. The total contribution from hypergraphs of this shape is~$O(N^{-v/3})$. Lemma~\ref{lem:higher-support-mass} proves a bound after summing over all shapes and labels, including the external field factors.

\subsection{Restricting the number of vertices}\label{subsec:higher-support}

For integers~$0\leq k\leq N$ and~$1\leq v\leq N$, define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:higher-retained-sum}
T_{N,k}=\sum_{\substack{\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal E_N\\|V(\Gamma)|\leq k}}W_h(\Gamma),
\qquad M_{N,v}=\sum_{\substack{\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal E_N\\|V(\Gamma)|=v}}
\hh^{2|\partial\Gamma|}\prod_{e\in\Gamma}r_{N,|e|}.
\end{equation}
Here~$T_{N,k}$ is the retained sum, and~$M_{N,v}$ is the sum of expected squared weights over hypergraphs using exactly~$v$ vertices. Lemma~\ref{lem:orthogonality} gives
\begin{equation*}
\E|\widehat Z_N-T_{N,k}|^2=\sum_{v=k+1}^N M_{N,v}.
\end{equation*}
Thus a bound on~$M_{N,v}$ will control the truncation error. Recall~$p_m\geq3$, let~$\rho_1=\max\{1,\rho_0\}$, and define
\begin{equation*}
\kappa_{\min}=\frac{p_m-2}{2p_m},\qquad
C_0=\exp\!\left(1+\sum_{p\in\mathcal P}\frac{\beta_0^2a_p^2}{p!}\right)\rho_1^2,
\qquad C_\star=C_0^{1/\kappa_{\min}}.
\end{equation*}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:higher-support-mass}
Let~$\alpha\geq1/4$ and define
\begin{equation*}
\kappa_\alpha=\min\left\{\frac{p_m-2}{p_m},\,2\alpha-\frac1{p_m}\right\}.
\end{equation*}
If~$|\hh|\leq\rho_1N^{-\alpha}$, then for every integer~$1\leq v\leq N$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:higher-support-mass}
M_{N,v}\leq C_0^v(v/N)^{\kappa_\alpha v}
\leq(C_\star v/N)^{\kappa_\alpha v}.
\end{equation}
At~$h=0$, the same bound holds with exponent~$(p_m-2)/p_m$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
There are two sources of decay. For a fixed number of used vertices, a low total degree forces many odd-degree vertices and therefore many small external field factors. A higher total degree instead gives decay through the edge second moments. We first bound this tradeoff for fixed edge counts, then sum over those counts. Fix~$v$ and nonnegative integers~$(n_p)_{p\in\mathcal P}$. Consider hypergraphs~$\Gamma$ with~$|V(\Gamma)|=v$ and exactly~$n_p$ edges of order~$p$. For edge counts admitting such a graph, the total degree is~$d=\sum_{p\in\mathcal P}pn_p\geq v$. Every used even-degree vertex has degree at least two, so~$d\geq2v-|\partial\Gamma|$. Let~$\ell=(2v-d)_+$, where~$x_+=\max\{x,0\}$. Then~$|\partial\Gamma|\geq\ell$ and~$0\leq\ell\leq v$. Since~$|\hh|\leq1$ and~$|\hh|\leq\rho_1N^{-\alpha}$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:higher-external-field-factor}
\hh^{2|\partial\Gamma|}
\leq |\hh|^{2\ell}
\leq \rho_1^{2\ell}N^{-2\alpha\ell}
\leq \rho_1^{2v}(v/N)^{2\alpha\ell}.
\end{equation}
The last inequality uses~$\rho_1\geq1$, $\ell\leq v$, and~$v\geq1$.
There are at most~$\binom Nv\prod_p\binom{\binom vp}{n_p}$ choices of vertices and edges. This count may include edge selections that leave some chosen vertices unused, so it is an upper bound. For every graph under consideration, the edge second moments have product~$\prod_p r_{N,p}^{n_p}$. Multiplying the count by this product and the upper bound in~\eqref{eq:higher-external-field-factor} gives a total contribution at most
\begin{align*}
&\binom Nv\rho_1^{2v}(v/N)^{2\alpha\ell}
\prod_{p\in\mathcal P}\binom{\binom vp}{n_p}r_{N,p}^{n_p}\\
&\qquad\leq(eN/v)^v\rho_1^{2v}
(v/N)^{2\alpha(2v-d)_++\sum_p(p-1)n_p}
\prod_{p\in\mathcal P}\frac{(\beta_0^2a_p^2v/p!)^{n_p}}{n_p!}.
\end{align*}
Here we used~\eqref{eq:edge-second-moment}, $\binom Nv\leq(eN/v)^v$, and~$\binom{\binom vp}{n_p}\leq(v^p/p!)^{n_p}/n_p!$. The identity~$v^pN^{1-p}=v(v/N)^{p-1}$ gives the displayed powers of~$v/N$.
We next show that the exponent of~$v/N$ is at least~$\kappa_\alpha v$ for every choice of nonnegative integers~$(n_p)_{p\in\mathcal P}$ satisfying~$\sum_{p\in\mathcal P}pn_p\geq v$. The exponent of~$v/N$ in the preceding bound on the sum of second moments is
\begin{equation*}
-v+\sum_{p\in\mathcal P}(p-1)n_p+2\alpha(2v-d)_+.
\end{equation*}
For every~$p\in\mathcal P$, $(p-1)/p\geq(p_m-1)/p_m$, so~$\sum_{p\in\mathcal P}(p-1)n_p\geq(p_m-1)d/p_m$. This exponent is therefore at least~$g(d)$, where we define
\begin{equation*}
g(d)=-v+\frac{p_m-1}{p_m}d+2\alpha(2v-d)_+.
\end{equation*}
This function is affine on~$[v,2v]$ and increasing after~$2v$. Its minimum is therefore the smaller of~$g(v)=(2\alpha-1/p_m)v$ and~$g(2v)=(p_m-2)v/p_m$, which is~$\kappa_\alpha v$.

We now sum the bounds for graphs with fixed edge counts to estimate~$M_{N,v}$. Since~$v/N\leq1$, the preceding lower bound on the exponent gives
\begin{align*}
M_{N,v}
&\leq e^v\rho_1^{2v}(v/N)^{\kappa_\alpha v}
\prod_{p\in\mathcal P}\left(\sum_{n_p=0}^{\infty}
\frac{(\beta_0^2a_p^2v/p!)^{n_p}}{n_p!}\right)\\
&=e^v\rho_1^{2v}(v/N)^{\kappa_\alpha v}
\exp\!\left(v\sum_{p\in\mathcal P}\frac{\beta_0^2a_p^2}{p!}\right)\\
&=C_0^v(v/N)^{\kappa_\alpha v}.
\end{align*}
In the first line, each~$n_p$ ranges independently over all nonnegative integers. This includes edge counts that cannot occur for a hypergraph with~$v$ used vertices, which only increases the sum because every term is nonnegative. The first equality follows by summing the exponential series for each~$p\in\mathcal P$.

For the second inequality in~\eqref{eq:higher-support-mass}, $C_\star^{\kappa_\alpha}=C_0^{\kappa_\alpha/\kappa_{\min}}\geq C_0$, since~$\kappa_\alpha\geq\kappa_{\min}$ and~$C_0\geq1$. At~$h=0$, only graphs with no odd-degree vertices contribute. They have~$d\geq2v$, which gives the stated exponent.
\end{proof}

The exponent reaches its value at zero external field when~$\alpha\geq(p_m-1)/(2p_m)$. The same threshold appears in the results of Dey and Wu~\cite{DW26}*{Corollary~2.4} on free energy fluctuations for mixed~$p$-spin models. For~$h\ne0$, define~$\alpha_h=\log(\rho_1/|\hh|)/\log N$. Then~$|\hh|=\rho_1N^{-\alpha_h}$. The external field assumption~\eqref{eq:field-range} gives~$|\hh|\leq|h|\leq\rho_0N^{-1/4}\leq\rho_1N^{-1/4}$, hence~$\alpha_h\geq1/4$. Applying the lemma with~$\alpha=\alpha_h$ gives the following exponent, which is computable from the supplied~$N,h$.

\begin{equation}\label{eq:field-dependent-kappa}
\kappa=\begin{cases}
\displaystyle\min\left\{\frac{p_m-2}{p_m},\,
\frac{2\log(\rho_1/|\hh|)}{\log N}-\frac1{p_m}\right\},&h\ne0,\\[6pt]
(p_m-2)/p_m,&h=0.
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
Thus~$\kappa_{\min}\leq\kappa\leq(p_m-2)/p_m$. To bound the mean-square truncation error~$\sum_{v=k+1}^{N}M_{N,v}$ given in~\eqref{eq:truncation-isometry}, define
\begin{equation*}
\Phi_N(t)=\kappa t\log\frac{N}{C_\star t}\quad(t>0),
\qquad \Phi_N(0)=0.
\end{equation*}
Lemma~\ref{lem:higher-support-mass} gives~$M_{N,v}\leq e^{-\Phi_N(v)}$, but this upper bound need not be small when~$v$ is comparable to~$N$. Choose a fixed~$\gamma>0$ small enough that~$e^{-\Phi_N(v)}$ decreases geometrically for~$v\leq\gamma N$. We can then bound~$\sum_{k<v\leq\gamma N}M_{N,v}$ by a geometric series. To bound~$\sum_{v>\gamma N}M_{N,v}$, we use the edge-tail estimate~\eqref{eq:edge-tail}, since every hypergraph with more than~$\gamma N$ vertices has more than~$\gamma N/P$ edges. Specifically, choose~$\gamma\in(0,1/4)$ such that
\begin{equation*}
\kappa_{\min}\left(\log\frac{1}{C_\star\gamma}-1\right)\geq2.
\end{equation*}
This ensures~$\Phi_N'(t)=\kappa(\log(N/(C_\star t))-1)\geq2$ for~$0<t\leq\gamma N$, giving the required geometric decrease. The upper limit~$1/4$ is only a convenient restriction. Section~\ref{subsec:cutoff-selection} gives an explicit choice of~$\gamma$, depending only on the fixed model and~$\beta_0,\rho_0$.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:higher-truncation}
For every integer~$0\leq k<\lfloor\gamma N\rfloor$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:higher-truncation}
\E|\widehat Z_N-T_{N,k}|^2\leq2e^{-\Phi_N(k+1)}+C_+\theta^{2\gamma N/P}.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
By~\eqref{eq:truncation-isometry}, the error is~$\sum_{v>k}M_{N,v}$. We split this sum at~$\lfloor\gamma N\rfloor$. For~$k<v\leq\lfloor\gamma N\rfloor$, the derivative bound gives~$\Phi_N(v)\geq\Phi_N(k+1)+2(v-k-1)$. Hence
\begin{equation*}
\sum_{v=k+1}^{\lfloor\gamma N\rfloor}M_{N,v}
\leq e^{-\Phi_N(k+1)}\sum_{j\geq0}e^{-2j}
=\frac{e^{-\Phi_N(k+1)}}{1-e^{-2}}
\leq2e^{-\Phi_N(k+1)}.
\end{equation*}
Every remaining graph has more than~$\gamma N$ vertices and hence more than~$\gamma N/P$ edges, since each edge contains at most~$P$ vertices. The edge-tail bound~\eqref{eq:edge-tail} gives the second term.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Exact summation on small vertex sets}\label{subsec:pure-evaluation}

We now evaluate~$T_{N,k}$ without enumerating its hypergraphs. For~$S\subseteq[N]$, define the local spin average
\begin{equation}\label{eq:local-spin-average}
G(S)=\frac{1}{(2\cosh h)^{|S|}}\sum_{\sigma\in\{-1,1\}^{S}}e^{h\sum_{i\in S}\sigma_i}
\prod_{\substack{e\in\mathcal E_N\\e\subseteq S}}(1+\omega_e\sigma_e).
\end{equation}
 This spin formula can be evaluated with~$2^{|S|}$ configurations, without listing the hypergraphs on~$S$. Expanding the product gives
\begin{equation*}
G(S)=\sum_{\substack{\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal E_N\\V(\Gamma)\subseteq S}}W_h(\Gamma).
\end{equation*}
Thus~$G(\varnothing)=1$ and~$G([N])=\widehat Z_N$. Summing~$G(S)$ over~$|S|\leq k$ can count the same hypergraph more than once. To correct this overcounting, we add and subtract the values~$G(S)$ using coefficients~$c_{N,k}(|S|)$. For integers~$0\leq s\leq k<N$, define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:exact-support-weight}
c_{N,k}(s)=(-1)^{k-s}\binom{N-s-1}{k-s}.
\end{equation}

The following Proposition~\ref{prop:pure-evaluation} proves that these coefficients cancel the extra occurrences of each graph weight, so every hypergraph using at most~$k$ vertices is counted exactly once. Algorithm~\ref{alg:support-sum} sums~$c_{N,k}(|S|)G(S)$ over all~$S\subseteq[N]$ with~$|S|\leq k$.

\begin{algorithm}[!htbp]
\caption{\textsc{SupportSum}$(N,h,\omega,k)$}\label{alg:support-sum}
\begin{algorithmic}[1]
\Require Cached interaction weights~$(\omega_e)$, $p_m\geq3$, external field~$h$, integer~$0\leq k\leq N$.
\If{$k=N$}
  \State \Return $G([N])$ from~\eqref{eq:local-spin-average}.
\EndIf
\State Precompute~$c_{N,k}(s)$ for~$0\leq s\leq k$, and initialize~$T\gets0$.
\For{each $S\subseteq[N]$ with~$|S|\leq k$, generated by cardinality}
  \State Evaluate~$G(S)$ and add~$c_{N,k}(|S|)G(S)$ to~$T$.
\EndFor
\State \Return $T$.
\end{algorithmic}
\end{algorithm}

The summation identity below is algebraic. For arbitrary finite real edge weights, define~$W_h$ and~$T_{N,k}$ by the same formulas for sums over graphs. No positivity or disorder assumption is needed for this identity.

\begin{proposition}[Exact summation of the vertex truncation]\label{prop:pure-evaluation}
For every finite real weight array, external field~$h\in\mathbb R$, and integer~$0\leq k<N$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:pure-exact-support}
T_{N,k}=\sum_{\substack{S\subseteq[N]\\|S|\leq k}}c_{N,k}(|S|)G(S).
\end{equation}
For~$k=N$, we have~$T_{N,N}=G([N])$. Algorithm~\ref{alg:support-sum} returns~$T_{N,k}$ exactly for every~$0\leq k\leq N$, using at most
\begin{equation}\label{eq:pure-evaluation-cost}
C_P(N+1)^{P+2}\sum_{s=0}^{k}\binom Ns2^s
\end{equation}
operations, including set generation and weight lookup. The constant~$C_P$ depends only on~$P$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For~$k<N$, fix~$U\subseteq[N]$. We first establish the identity
\begin{equation*}
\sum_{S\subseteq U}(-1)^{|U|-|S|}G(S)
=\sum_{V(\Gamma)=U}W_h(\Gamma).
\end{equation*}
Expanding~$G(S)$ and interchanging the finite sums gives
\begin{align*}
\sum_{S\subseteq U}(-1)^{|U|-|S|}G(S)
&=\sum_{S\subseteq U}\sum_{V(\Gamma)\subseteq U}
(-1)^{|U|-|S|}\ind_{\{V(\Gamma)\subseteq S\}}W_h(\Gamma)\\
&=\sum_{V(\Gamma)\subseteq U}W_h(\Gamma)
\sum_{V(\Gamma)\subseteq S\subseteq U}(-1)^{|U|-|S|}.
\end{align*}
For a fixed graph, write~$X=V(\Gamma)$ and~$m=|U\setminus X|$. Since~$S$ must contain~$X$, it can omit vertices only from~$U\setminus X$. Omitting exactly~$j$ of these vertices gives~$\binom mj$ choices of~$S$, each with~$|U|-|S|=j$. Therefore,
\begin{equation*}
\sum_{X\subseteq S\subseteq U}(-1)^{|U|-|S|}
=\sum_{j=0}^{m}\binom mj(-1)^j.
\end{equation*}
If~$m>0$, this sum equals~$(1-1)^m=0$ by the binomial theorem. If~$m=0$, only~$S=U=X$ contributes, giving one. Thus only graphs with~$V(\Gamma)=U$ remain, proving the identity. Summing this identity over~$|U|\leq k$ gives~$T_{N,k}$, since each retained graph appears once. Interchanging the finite sums once more gives
\begin{align*}
T_{N,k}
=\sum_{\substack{U\subseteq[N]\\|U|\leq k}}
\sum_{S\subseteq U}(-1)^{|U|-|S|}G(S)
=\sum_{\substack{S\subseteq[N]\\|S|\leq k}}G(S)
\sum_{\substack{S\subseteq U\subseteq[N]\\|U|\leq k}}(-1)^{|U|-|S|}.
\end{align*}
For a fixed~$S$, write~$s=|S|$. A superset~$U$ of size~$s+j$ is obtained by adding~$j$ vertices from~$[N]\setminus S$, giving~$\binom{N-s}{j}$ choices, each with sign~$(-1)^j$. The multiplier of~$G(S)$ is therefore~$\sum_{j=0}^{k-s}(-1)^j\binom{N-s}{j}$. Applying Pascal's identity and shifting the index in the second sum yields
\begin{align*}
\sum_{j=0}^{k-s}(-1)^j\binom{N-s}{j}
&=\sum_{j=0}^{k-s}(-1)^j\binom{N-s-1}{j}
-\sum_{j=0}^{k-s-1}(-1)^j\binom{N-s-1}{j}\\
&=(-1)^{k-s}\binom{N-s-1}{k-s}
=c_{N,k}(s).
\end{align*}
The second sum is empty when~$s=k$. Substituting~$c_{N,k}(|S|)$ for the multiplier of~$G(S)$ proves~\eqref{eq:pure-exact-support}. For~$k=N$, both~$G([N])$ and~$T_{N,N}$ sum all graph weights, so they are equal.

It remains to bound the work of evaluating each local average once. Precompute the coefficients by Pascal's recurrence in~$O(N^2)$ operations. A standard fixed-size subset recursion generates all sets of size~$s$ in~$O((N+1)^2\binom Ns)$ operations, including their vertex lists. For each set, enumerate its~$2^s$ spin assignments. Store membership and spins in an array indexed by~$[N]$, then scan the interaction list. It has at most~$C_P(N+1)^P$ entries, each involving at most~$P$ sites. Computing the external field weight and normalization takes another~$O(N)$ operations per assignment. Thus generation, containment tests, spin products, weight access, and the external field factors cost at most~$C_P(N+1)^{P+1}$ per assignment. Summing proves~\eqref{eq:pure-evaluation-cost}, including preparation and the direct evaluation when~$k=N$.
\end{proof}

Section~\ref{subsec:cutoff-selection} uses the mean-square error bound~\eqref{eq:higher-truncation} and the bound~\eqref{eq:pure-evaluation-cost} on the number of operations needed to compute~$T_{N,k}$ to choose~$k$ so that the requested accuracy is achieved with a polynomial operation count.

\section{The SK model}\label{sec:sk}

Building on the cycle expansion of Aizenman, Lebowitz, and Ruelle~\cite{ALR87}, Dey and Wu~\cite{DW23} identify simple cycles and paths as the leading clusters for~$\widehat Z_N$ at high temperature under weak external fields. For fixed~$\beta>0$, the total contribution of cycles of any fixed length~$\ell\geq3$ remains of order one in~$L^2$ as~$N\to\infty$. Paths of any fixed length~$\ell\geq1$ also have a total contribution of order one when~$h=\rho N^{-1/4}$ with fixed~$\rho\ne0$. Thus graphs using a fixed number of vertices can still make a nonvanishing contribution, so the decay with~$N$ in~\eqref{eq:higher-support-mass} is unavailable in SK.

We instead bound the number of branching vertices, those of degree at least three, and separately limit the number of edges. Section~\ref{subsec:sk-support} controls the error. Sections~\ref{subsec:sk-evaluation} and~\ref{subsec:sk-colorful} decompose graphs with a fixed branching set into paths and cycles, then sum them by color coding. Section~\ref{subsec:sk-estimator} averages the resulting estimates and bounds their error and cost.

\subsection{Branching truncation}\label{subsec:sk-support}

Write~$\omega_{ij}=\omega_{\{i,j\}}$ for distinct~$i,j\in[N]$ and~$\omega_{ii}=0$. Here~$\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal E_{N,2}$ is a selected set of pair interactions, and all its degrees are computed using only these edges. Define its branching set by
\begin{equation*}
\Br(\Gamma)=\{i\in[N]:\deg_\Gamma(i)\geq3\}.
\end{equation*}
For integers~$0\leq D\leq N$ and~$L\geq1$, define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sk-retained-sum}
T^{\mathrm{SK}}_{N,D,L}=\sum_{\substack{\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal E_N\\|\Br(\Gamma)|\leq D,\ |\Gamma|\leq L}}W_h(\Gamma).
\end{equation}
For~$0\leq b\leq N$, define the sum of expected squared weights over graphs with exactly~$b$ branching vertices by
\begin{align*}
M^{\mathrm{SK}}_{N,b}
&=\sum_{\substack{\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal E_N\\|\Br(\Gamma)|=b}}\E W_h(\Gamma)^2=\sum_{\substack{\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal E_N\\|\Br(\Gamma)|=b}}
\hh^{2|\partial\Gamma|}r_{N,2}^{|\Gamma|}.
\end{align*}
This sum fixes only the number of branching vertices and imposes no edge cutoff. The external field factor is squared. Thus two odd-degree vertices contribute~$\hh^4\leq\rho_0^4/N$ to each expected squared weight.

% BEGIN ADDED FIGURE 1
Figure~\ref{fig:sk-branching-scales} illustrates the second-moment scales for three example graph shapes.
\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\manuscriptFigure{main-12-2-2-figures/sk-branching-scales.pdf}{\manuscriptBranchingFigure}
\caption{Branching and second-moment decay. Here $b=|\Br(\Gamma)|$. Each bound concerns the sum of expected squared weights over all labeled copies of the displayed shape, under $|h|\leq\rho_0N^{-1/4}$. Attaching the separate edge to the triangle removes one free vertex label without changing the two odd-degree vertices, whose squared external field factor is $\widehat h^4=O(N^{-1})$. Two triangles sharing one vertex have one more edge than vertices and also give $O(N^{-1})$.}
\label{fig:sk-branching-scales}
\end{figure}
% END ADDED FIGURE 1

Recall the constants~$C_+$ and~$\theta$ defined in~\eqref{eq:algorithm-mass-constant} and~\eqref{eq:algorithm-temperature-constants}, respectively, where~$\beta_{\mathrm f}=1$ and~$\Delta=1/2$ for SK. For the branching bound, define
\begin{align}\label{eq:sk-mass-constants}
C_{\mathrm{SK}}=\sqrt{\frac2{1-\beta_0^2}}\exp\!\left(\frac{\rho_0^4\beta_0^2}{1-\beta_0^2}\right),\quad
A_{\mathrm{SK}}=\frac{128e}{(1-\beta_0^2)^2}
\left(\frac{\rho_0^2\beta_0^3}{6}+\frac{\beta_0^4}{24}\right),
\quad C_\star=\max\{1,A_{\mathrm{SK}}\}.
\end{align}
For SK, set~$\kappa=\kappa_{\min}=1$ and define
\begin{equation*}
\Phi_N(t)=t\log\frac{N}{C_\star t}\quad(t>0),
\qquad \Phi_N(0)=0.
\end{equation*}

To estimate the mean-square error from the branching cutoff, we first bound~$M^{\mathrm{SK}}_{N,b}$ for each number~$b$ of branching vertices.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:sk-branching-mass}
For every integer~$1\leq b\leq N$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sk-branching-mass}
M^{\mathrm{SK}}_{N,b}\leq C_{\mathrm{SK}}\left(\frac{A_{\mathrm{SK}}b}{N}\right)^b
\leq C_{\mathrm{SK}}e^{-\Phi_N(b)}.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
We first count graphs with prescribed degrees, then sum over the permitted degrees. The calculation is related to~\cite{DW23}*{Lemma~4.1}. For any nonnegative integer~$d$, write
\begin{equation*}
\widetilde d=d\bmod2\in\{0,1\}.
\end{equation*}
Fix~$A\subseteq[N]$ with~$|A|=b$. Consider simple graphs with branching set~$A$ and prescribed degrees~$d_1,\ldots,d_N$, and let~$m$ be their number of edges. Then
\begin{equation*}
d_i\geq3\quad(i\in A),\qquad
d_i\in\{0,1,2\}\quad(i\in[N]\setminus A),\qquad
\sum_{i=1}^N d_i=2m.
\end{equation*}
The counting argument in~\cite{FK24}*{Lemma~11.1 and (11.1)} bounds the number of simple graphs with these degrees by
\(
\frac{(2m-1)!!}{\prod_{i=1}^N d_i!}.
\)
Each such graph contributes~$r_{N,2}^{m}\prod_i\hh^{\,2\widetilde d_i}$ to~$M^{\mathrm{SK}}_{N,b}$.

To sum the counting bound over degree sequences, we write it as the expectation of a product with one factor for each vertex. Let~$z$ be an auxiliary standard Gaussian, and write~$\E_z$ for its expectation. Since~$\E_z|z|^{2m}=(2m-1)!!$, the contribution of graphs with this degree sequence is bounded by
\begin{equation*}
\frac{(2m-1)!!\,r_{N,2}^{m}}{\prod_i d_i!}
\prod_i\hh^{\,2\widetilde d_i}
=\E_z\prod_{i=1}^N
\frac{\hh^{\,2\widetilde d_i}r_{N,2}^{d_i/2}|z|^{d_i}}{d_i!}.
\end{equation*}
We now sum over all~$d_i\geq3$ for~$i\in A$ and all~$d_i\in\{0,1,2\}$ for~$i\in[N]\setminus A$, without requiring an even total degree or a sequence realized by a simple graph. This only adds nonnegative terms. Tonelli's theorem allows us to interchange the degree sums with~$\E_z$ and sum separately over the degree at each vertex. To bound these sums at~$x=\sqrt{r_{N,2}}\,|z|$, we use the following inequalities, valid for every~$x\geq0$. For a vertex in~$[N]\setminus A$,
\begin{equation*}
\sum_{d=0}^{2}\frac{\hh^{\,2\widetilde d}x^d}{d!}
=1+\hh^2x+\frac{x^2}{2}
\leq e^{\hh^2x+x^2/2}.
\end{equation*}
For a vertex in~$A$, separating odd and even degrees gives
\begin{align*}
\sum_{d\geq3}\frac{\hh^{\,2\widetilde d}x^d}{d!}
=\hh^2\sum_{j\geq0}\frac{x^{2j+3}}{(2j+3)!}
+\sum_{j\geq0}\frac{x^{2j+4}}{(2j+4)!}
\leq\left(\frac{\hh^2x^3}{6}+\frac{x^4}{24}\right)e^{x^2/2}.
\end{align*}
Here we used~$(2j+3)!\geq6\,2^jj!$ and~$(2j+4)!\geq24\,2^jj!$. Applying the bounds for the two degree sums, using~$(N-b)\hh^2x\leq N\hh^2x$, and summing over the~$\binom Nb$ choices of~$A$, we obtain
\begin{equation*}
M^{\mathrm{SK}}_{N,b}\leq\binom Nb\,\E_z\!\left[
\left(\frac{\hh^2r_{N,2}^{3/2}|z|^3}{6}+\frac{r_{N,2}^{2}z^4}{24}\right)^b
 e^{N\hh^2\sqrt{r_{N,2}}\,|z|+Nr_{N,2}z^2/2}\right].
\end{equation*}

We next estimate this expectation. The bounds~$r_{N,2}\leq\beta_0^2/N$ and~$\hh^2\leq\rho_0^2N^{-1/2}$ imply
\begin{equation*}
\frac{\hh^2r_{N,2}^{3/2}|z|^3}{6}+\frac{r_{N,2}^{2}z^4}{24}
\leq\frac{1}{N^2}
\left(\frac{\rho_0^2\beta_0^3}{6}+\frac{\beta_0^4}{24}\right)(1+z^4),
\end{equation*}
where we used~$|z|^3\leq1+z^4$. To keep the Gaussian integral finite, we bound the exponent by a constant plus a quadratic term whose coefficient is below~$1/2$. Set
\begin{equation*}
\eta=\frac{1-\beta_0^2}{2}\in(0,1/2].
\end{equation*}
Completing the square gives
\begin{align*}
N\hh^2\sqrt{r_{N,2}}\,|z|+\frac{Nr_{N,2}}2z^2
\leq\rho_0^2\beta_0|z|+\frac{\beta_0^2}{2}z^2
\leq\frac{\rho_0^4\beta_0^2}{1-\beta_0^2}+\frac{1-\eta}{2}z^2.
\end{align*}

Multiplying the Gaussian density by~$e^{(1-\eta)z^2/2}$ leaves~$e^{-\eta z^2/2}/\sqrt{2\pi}$. Rescaling the integral therefore yields
\begin{align*}
\E_z\!\left[(1+z^4)^b e^{(1-\eta)z^2/2}\right]
&=\eta^{-1/2}\E_z(1+\eta^{-2}z^4)^b\\
&\leq\eta^{-1/2}2^{b-1}\left(1+\eta^{-2b}(4b-1)!!\right)
\leq\eta^{-1/2}\left(\frac{32b^2}{\eta^2}\right)^b.
\end{align*}
The inequalities use~$(1+t)^b\leq2^{b-1}(1+t^b)$ for~$t\geq0$, the Gaussian moment formula, and~$(4b-1)!!\leq(4b)^{2b}$, together with~$b\geq1$ and~$\eta\leq1$.
Combining these estimates and using the definition of~$C_{\mathrm{SK}}$, we have
\begin{equation*}
M^{\mathrm{SK}}_{N,b}\leq C_{\mathrm{SK}}\binom Nb
\left(\frac{\rho_0^2\beta_0^3/6+\beta_0^4/24}{N^2}\right)^b
\left(\frac{32b^2}{\eta^2}\right)^b.
\end{equation*}
Now~$\binom Nb\leq(eN/b)^b$ and
\(
\frac{32e}{\eta^2}
\left(\frac{\rho_0^2\beta_0^3}{6}+\frac{\beta_0^4}{24}\right)
=A_{\mathrm{SK}},
\)
so
\(
M^{\mathrm{SK}}_{N,b}\leq C_{\mathrm{SK}}\left(\frac{A_{\mathrm{SK}}b}{N}\right)^b.
\)
Finally, $A_{\mathrm{SK}}\leq C_\star$ gives
\begin{equation*}
\left(\frac{A_{\mathrm{SK}}b}{N}\right)^b
\leq\left(\frac{C_\star b}{N}\right)^b
=e^{-\Phi_N(b)}.
\end{equation*}
the proof is complete.
\end{proof}

We now combine the branching bound with the edge-tail estimate. Choose~$\gamma\in(0,1/4)$ with~$\log(1/(C_\star\gamma))-1\geq2$. Then~$\Phi_N'\geq2$ on~$(0,\gamma N]$, so the branching bounds can be summed geometrically in this range. More than~$\gamma N$ branching vertices force more than~$3\gamma N/2$ edges, where we use the edge-tail bound instead.

\begin{proposition}[SK truncation]\label{prop:sk-truncation}
For integers~$0\leq D<\lfloor\gamma N\rfloor$ and~$L\geq1$, let~$T^{\mathrm{SK}}_{N,D,L}$ be the sum in~\eqref{eq:sk-retained-sum}, retaining exactly the graphs with at most~$D$ branching vertices and at most~$L$ original edges. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sk-truncation}
\E|\widehat Z_N-T^{\mathrm{SK}}_{N,D,L}|^2
\leq C_+\theta^{2(L+1)}+2C_{\mathrm{SK}}e^{-\Phi_N(D+1)}+C_+\theta^{3\gamma N}.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Let~$B=\lfloor\gamma N\rfloor$. A graph is discarded if it has more than~$L$ edges, or if it has at most~$L$ edges but more than~$D$ branching vertices. These alternatives are disjoint. Orthogonality therefore gives
\begin{align*}
\E|\widehat Z_N-T^{\mathrm{SK}}_{N,D,L}|^2
&=\sum_{|\Gamma|>L}\E W_h(\Gamma)^2
 +\sum_{\substack{|\Gamma|\leq L\\|\Br(\Gamma)|>D}}\E W_h(\Gamma)^2\\
&\leq\sum_{|\Gamma|>L}\E W_h(\Gamma)^2
 +\sum_{b=D+1}^{B}M^{\mathrm{SK}}_{N,b}
 +\sum_{|\Br(\Gamma)|>B}\E W_h(\Gamma)^2.
\end{align*}
In the inequality we removed the edge restriction from the second sum and split it at branching count~$B$. All summands are nonnegative.

The first sum is at most~$C_+\theta^{2(L+1)}$ by~\eqref{eq:edge-tail}. For the middle sum, $D+1\leq b\leq B$ and~$\Phi_N'\geq2$ give
$\Phi_N(b)-\Phi_N(D+1)\geq2(b-D-1)$. Lemma~\ref{lem:sk-branching-mass} consequently yields
\begin{equation*}
\sum_{b=D+1}^{B}M^{\mathrm{SK}}_{N,b}
\leq C_{\mathrm{SK}}e^{-\Phi_N(D+1)}\sum_{j=0}^{B-D-1}e^{-2j}
\leq2C_{\mathrm{SK}}e^{-\Phi_N(D+1)}.
\end{equation*}
For the last sum, the integer inequality~$|\Br(\Gamma)|>B$ implies~$|\Br(\Gamma)|>\gamma N$. Each branching vertex has degree at least three, so
\begin{equation*}
2|\Gamma|=\sum_{i=1}^N\deg_\Gamma(i)
\geq3|\Br(\Gamma)|>3\gamma N.
\end{equation*}
Thus this family is contained in~$\{|\Gamma|>3\gamma N/2\}$, whose total expected squared weight is at most~$C_+\theta^{3\gamma N}$ by~\eqref{eq:edge-tail}. Adding the three estimates proves~\eqref{eq:sk-truncation}.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Augmented graphs}\label{subsec:sk-evaluation}

To evaluate~$T^{\mathrm{SK}}_{N,D,L}$, we group graphs by their exact branching set. We first represent the external field factor by extra edges. Adjoin a vertex~$0$, assign~$\omega_{0i}=\omega_{i0}=\hh$ for~$i\in[N]$, and define the augmented graph on~$\{0,1,\ldots,N\}$ by
\begin{equation*}
\Gamma^+=\Gamma\cup\bigl\{\{0,i\}:i\in\partial\Gamma\bigr\}.
\end{equation*}
All original edges are kept. The added edges give
\begin{equation*}
\prod_{e\in\Gamma^+}\omega_e=W_h(\Gamma),\qquad
\deg_{\Gamma^+}(i)=\deg_\Gamma(i)+\ind_{\{i\in\partial\Gamma\}}\quad(i\in[N]).
\end{equation*}
The degree in~$\Gamma^+$ of every original vertex is even. The degree at~$0$ is~$|\partial\Gamma|$, which is even by the sum-of-degrees identity. Conversely, evenness at each original vertex uniquely determines whether its edge to~$0$ is present. Thus augmentation is a bijection between simple graphs on~$[N]$ and simple graphs on~$\{0,1,\ldots,N\}$ in which every degree is even, allowing isolated vertices in both cases. An original vertex is branching exactly when its augmented degree is at least four. Only original edges count toward~$L$.

Fix~$A\subseteq[N]$ and define
\begin{equation*}
A^+=A\cup\{0\},\qquad A^c=[N]\setminus A.
\end{equation*}
We call each vertex of~$A^c$ an \emph{outside vertex}. If~$\Br(\Gamma)=A$, then~$\deg_\Gamma(v)\in\{0,1,2\}$ for~$v\in A^c$. Augmentation changes these degrees to~$0,2,2$, respectively, so
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sk-outside-degree}
\deg_{\Gamma^+}(v)=2\qquad\bigl(v\in V(\Gamma^+)\cap A^c\bigr).
\end{equation}
This degree constraint yields a decomposition into the following two types of edge sets.

--\emph{Path pieces}. The collection~$\mathscr P_A$ of \emph{path pieces} consists of the edge sets
\begin{equation*}
\bigl\{\{v_{i-1},v_i\}:1\leq i\leq\ell\bigr\},\qquad \ell\geq1,
\end{equation*}
where~$v_0,\ldots,v_\ell\in\{0,1,\ldots,N\}$ are pairwise distinct and satisfy
\begin{equation*}
v_0,v_\ell\in A^+,\qquad \{v_1,\ldots,v_{\ell-1}\}\subseteq A^c.
\end{equation*}
Thus the endpoints are~$v_0\ne v_\ell$, and the internal vertices are~$v_1,\ldots,v_{\ell-1}$. The case~$\ell=1$ gives the singleton edge set~$\{\{a,b\}\}$ with~$a,b\in A^+$ and~$a\ne b$. We call~$\{a,b\}$ a \emph{direct edge}.

--\emph{Cycle pieces}. The collection~$\mathscr C_A$ of \emph{cycle pieces} consists of the edge sets
\begin{equation*}
\bigl\{\{v_{i-1},v_i\}:1\leq i\leq\ell\bigr\},\qquad
\ell\geq3,\quad v_\ell=v_0,
\end{equation*}
where~$v_0,\ldots,v_{\ell-1}\in\{0,1,\ldots,N\}$ are pairwise distinct and
\begin{equation*}
\bigl|\{v_0,\ldots,v_{\ell-1}\}\cap A^+\bigr|\leq1.
\end{equation*}
Members of~$\mathscr P_A$ and~$\mathscr C_A$ are undirected edge sets. Reversing a path, or changing the starting vertex or orientation of a cycle, gives the same piece. Figure~\ref{fig:sk-augmentation}(c) shows two path pieces and two cycle pieces for~$A=\{1\}$.

% BEGIN ADDED FIGURE 2
\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\manuscriptFigure{main-12-2-2-figures/sk-augmentation-decomposition.pdf}{\manuscriptAugmentationFigure}
\caption{Augmentation and the edge partition in Lemma~\ref{lem:sk-decomposition}. In (a), the branching set is $A=\{1\}$ and the odd-degree set is $\partial\Gamma=\{1,4\}$, marked by rings. In (b), two dashed edges to the auxiliary vertex $0$ supply the factor $\widehat h^2$. Row (c) contains exactly the same edges, separated into four pieces. Repeated labels $0$ and $1$ denote the same vertices of $A^+$, not new vertices. The outside vertex sets are disjoint. Only solid, original edges count toward $L$.}
\label{fig:sk-augmentation}
\end{figure}
% END ADDED FIGURE 2

\begin{lemma}[Path and cycle decomposition]\label{lem:sk-decomposition}
Let~$\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal E_{N,2}$ satisfy~$\Br(\Gamma)=A$. There is a unique collection~$\mathcal Q_\Gamma\subseteq\mathscr P_A\cup\mathscr C_A$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sk-piece-decomposition}
\Gamma^+=\bigsqcup_{Q\in\mathcal Q_\Gamma}Q,\qquad
V(Q)\cap V(Q')\subseteq A^+\quad
\text{for distinct }Q,Q'\in\mathcal Q_\Gamma.
\end{equation}
The disjoint union means that each edge of~$\Gamma^+$ belongs to exactly one piece. Conversely, let~$\mathcal Q\subseteq\mathscr P_A\cup\mathscr C_A$ satisfy
\begin{equation*}
Q\cap Q'=\varnothing,\qquad V(Q)\cap V(Q')\subseteq A^+
\quad\text{for distinct }Q,Q'\in\mathcal Q,
\end{equation*}
and set~$H=\bigsqcup_{Q\in\mathcal Q}Q$. Then~$H=\Gamma^+$ for a unique~$\Gamma$ with~$\Br(\Gamma)=A$ if and only if
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sk-degree-conditions}
\deg_H(v)\equiv0\pmod2\quad(v\in\{0,1,\ldots,N\}),\qquad
\deg_H(a)\geq4\quad(a\in A).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

The first part gives the unique path and cycle decomposition of~$\Gamma^+$ when~$\Br(\Gamma)=A$. The second part considers pieces that share no edges and meet only in~$A^+$. Their union equals~$\Gamma^+$ for a graph with~$\Br(\Gamma)=A$ if and only if~\eqref{eq:sk-degree-conditions} holds. These conditions require every degree to be even and each vertex of~$A$ to have degree at least four. In Section~\ref{subsec:sk-colorful}, we sum over these collections and impose the degree conditions. Uniqueness ensures that each graph is counted once.

\begin{proof}
Set
\begin{equation*}
U=V(\Gamma^+)\cap A^c,\qquad
G=\left(U,\Gamma^+\cap\binom{U}{2}\right),
\end{equation*}
and let~$\mathcal K$ be the collection of vertex sets of the connected components of~$G$, including isolated vertices. By~\eqref{eq:sk-outside-degree}, every vertex of~$G$ has degree at most two. Each component is therefore a cycle, a path of positive length, or an isolated vertex. For~$K\in\mathcal K$, define
\begin{equation*}
Q_K=\{e\in\Gamma^+:e\cap K\ne\varnothing\}.
\end{equation*}
If the component on~$K$ is a cycle, its vertices already have degree two within~$K$, so
\begin{equation*}
Q_K=\Gamma^+\cap\binom{K}{2}\in\mathscr C_A.
\end{equation*}
Otherwise, write~$K=\{u_1,\ldots,u_r\}$ in path order, allowing~$r=1$ for an isolated vertex. Equation~\eqref{eq:sk-outside-degree} supplies exactly two edges from~$K$ to~$A^+$, giving
\begin{equation*}
Q_K=\bigl\{\{u_i,u_{i+1}\}:1\leq i<r\bigr\}
\cup\bigl\{\{a,u_1\},\{u_r,b\}\bigr\},
\qquad a,b\in A^+.
\end{equation*}
When~$r=1$, the two neighbors~$a,b$ are distinct because~$\Gamma^+$ is simple. Hence~$Q_K\in\mathscr P_A$ if~$a\ne b$, and~$Q_K\in\mathscr C_A$ if~$a=b$. The latter case necessarily has~$r\geq2$. In Figure~\ref{fig:sk-augmentation}(b), the component sets~$\{2,3\}$, $\{4\}$, and~$\{5,6,7\}$ give, respectively, the cycle~$1,2,3,1$, the path~$1,4,0$, and the cycle~$5,6,7,5$ in part~(c).

Define
\begin{equation*}
\mathcal Q_\Gamma=
\bigl\{\{e\}:e\in\Gamma^+\cap\binom{A^+}{2}\bigr\}
\cup\{Q_K:K\in\mathcal K\}.
\end{equation*}
Every edge either has both endpoints in~$A^+$ or meets exactly one component set~$K$. Also~$V(Q_K)\cap A^c=K$, and distinct component sets are disjoint. These facts prove~\eqref{eq:sk-piece-decomposition}. In Figure~\ref{fig:sk-augmentation}, the only direct edge is~$\{0,1\}$.

For uniqueness, consider any collection satisfying~\eqref{eq:sk-piece-decomposition}. Both edges incident to a vertex~$v\in U$ must belong to the same piece, since~$v$ has degree two in every piece containing it. It follows along each component of~$G$ that all edges of~$Q_K$ belong to one piece. For a piece meeting~$A^c$, the subgraph induced by its vertices in~$A^c$ is connected. Thus this piece cannot contain vertices from two different component sets and is exactly~$Q_K$. Every remaining edge has both endpoints in~$A^+$ and can only be a singleton path piece. Thus~$\mathcal Q_\Gamma$ is unique.

For the converse, the pieces have disjoint edge sets and no common vertex in~$A^c$, so~$H$ is simple and
\begin{equation*}
\deg_H(v)\in\{0,2\}\qquad(v\in A^c).
\end{equation*}
The stated degree conditions are necessary by the augmentation identities. Suppose they hold and put~$\Gamma=H\cap\mathcal E_{N,2}$. For~$i\in[N]$,
\begin{equation*}
\deg_\Gamma(i)=\deg_H(i)-\ind_{\{\{0,i\}\in H\}}.
\end{equation*}
Since~$\deg_H(i)$ is even, this gives~$i\in\partial\Gamma$ if and only if~$\{0,i\}\in H$, and hence~$H=\Gamma^+$. Moreover,
\begin{equation*}
\deg_\Gamma(a)\geq4-1=3\quad(a\in A),\qquad
\deg_\Gamma(v)\leq2\quad(v\in A^c).
\end{equation*}
Thus~$\Br(\Gamma)=A$. The identity~$\Gamma=H\cap\mathcal E_{N,2}$ also proves uniqueness.
\end{proof}



\subsection{Color-coded summation}\label{subsec:sk-colorful}

Fix a candidate branching set~$A\subseteq[N]$ and an edge cutoff~$L\geq1$. Lemma~\ref{lem:sk-decomposition} reduces the graph sum to collections of paths and cycles, but listing all possible vertex sequences of these pieces would be too expensive.

\emph{Color coding} was introduced by Alon, Yuster, and Zwick~\cite{AYZ95} to find simple paths and cycles of a prescribed length in a graph. The difficulty is to extend a path without revisiting a vertex. Their idea is to color vertices randomly and consider paths whose vertices have distinct colors. Extending such a path to an unused color preserves simplicity, so one only needs to remember the colors already used. Here we sum together the weights of paths with the same endpoint and color set. This avoids enumerating their vertex sequences. The number of colors is controlled by~$L$. Cycles are handled similarly, as detailed below.

Lemma~\ref{lem:sk-decomposition} allows different pieces to share vertices in~$A^+$, but not in~$A^c$. We therefore leave~$A^+$ uncolored and fix a coloring~$\chi:A^c\to[2L]$. We use~$2L$ colors because any graph with at most~$L$ original edges uses at most~$2L$ vertices in~$A^c$. A graph or piece is called \emph{colorful} if the vertices it uses in~$A^c$ have distinct colors. To illustrate how we sum path weights, fix~$a\in A^+$ and~$j\in A^c$ with~$\chi(j)=2$. Consider all paths~$a,i,j$ with~$i\in A^c$ and~$\chi(i)=1$. If~$v\in A^c$ has a color other than~$1,2$, every such path extends to a simple path~$a,i,j,v$. Their total weight is
\begin{equation*}
\sum_{\substack{i\in A^c\\\chi(i)=1}}\omega_{ai}\omega_{ij}\omega_{jv}
=\Bigg(\sum_{\substack{i\in A^c\\\chi(i)=1}}\omega_{ai}\omega_{ij}\Bigg)\omega_{jv}.
\end{equation*}
The sum in parentheses is the total weight of the original paths. Multiplying it by~$\omega_{jv}$ computes the total weight of their extensions without processing each path separately.

We combine colorful pieces only if their vertices in~$A^c$ use no common color. This prevents different pieces from sharing a vertex in~$A^c$. For a fixed coloring, we thus count only colorful graphs. In Section~\ref{subsec:sk-estimator}, we divide each included graph's weight by its probability of being colorful under a uniformly random coloring. Averaging over independent colorings gives an unbiased estimate of the full retained sum. Here we compute the colorful graph sums exactly for a fixed~$\chi$.

We group colorful graphs by their branching set, number of edges, and colors used in~$A^c$. The weights of graphs in the same group will be added together.
For~$0\leq k\leq L$ and~$S\subseteq[2L]$, let~$\mathcal G_A^\chi(k,S)$ be the set of colorful graphs~$\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal E_{N,2}$ satisfying
\begin{equation*}
