\Br(\Gamma)=A,\qquad |\Gamma|=k,\qquad \chi\bigl(V(\Gamma)\setminus A\bigr)=S.
\end{equation*}
Thus this set contains exactly the colorful graphs with branching set~$A$, $k$ edges, and colors~$S$ on their used vertices in~$A^c$. Define their total weight by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sk-colorful-identity}
f_A^\chi(k,S):=\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathcal G_A^\chi(k,S)}W_h(\Gamma).
\end{equation}
Summing these quantities over~$k,S$ gives the colorful part of the contribution from~$A$ to~$T^{\mathrm{SK}}_{N,D,L}$ when~$|A|\leq D$.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:sk-colorful}
Let~$h\in\mathbb R$ be the external field, and let~$\omega_{ij}\in\mathbb R$ be the weight of the edge~$\{i,j\}$ for~$1\leq i<j\leq N$. For any candidate branching set~$A\subseteq[N]$, integer~$L\geq1$, and coloring~$\chi:A^c\to[2L]$, the construction below computes all sums~$f_A^\chi(k,S)$ in~\eqref{eq:sk-colorful-identity}, for~$0\leq k\leq L$ and~$S\subseteq[2L]$, exactly and deterministically. The total number of operations for all these sums is at most
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sk-colorful-cost}
C_{\mathrm{alg}}(N+2L+1)^6\,16^L\,36^{|A|}\,,
\end{equation}
where~$C_{\mathrm{alg}}$ is an absolute constant.
\end{proposition}

This exact evaluation holds for any external field and any real edge weights. The restriction on the external field~$|h|\leq\rho_0N^{-1/4}$ is used in the truncation and mean-square error estimates. To compute these sums, we first sum the weights of individual path and cycle pieces. We combine pieces only if their vertices in~$A^c$ use no common color. We keep only unions satisfying~\eqref{eq:sk-degree-conditions}. Thus every vertex must have even degree, and every vertex of~$A$ must have degree at least four.

\paragraph{Weights of paths and cycles.}
Here we compute the total weights of colorful path and cycle pieces that meet~$A^c$. We group the pieces by the colors of their vertices in~$A^c$ and the vertices they meet in~$A^+$. Recall from Section~\ref{subsec:sk-evaluation} that a \emph{direct edge} joins two distinct vertices of~$A^+$ and therefore has no vertex in~$A^c$. These sums, together with the weights of direct edges, will be used in the next part to compute~$f_A^\chi(k,S)$ by combining pieces.

Give each auxiliary edge~$\{0,i\}$ weight~$\omega_{0i}=\omega_{i0}=\hh=\tanh h$. The weight of a path or cycle is the product of its edge weights. For~$a\in A^+$, $j\in A^c$, and~$S\subseteq[2L]$, define~$P_a(S,j)$ to be the sum of weights of paths from~$a$ to~$j$ whose vertices after~$a$ lie in~$A^c$ and have exactly the colors in~$S$, each once. The preceding example with two colors is the entry~$P_a(\{1,2\},j)$.

The initial entries are~$P_a(\{\chi(j)\},j)=\omega_{aj}$. Entries with~$S=\varnothing$ or~$\chi(j)\notin S$ are zero. For~$|S|\geq2$ and~$\chi(j)\in S$, compute
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sk-path-recurrence}
P_a(S,j)=\sum_{\substack{i\in A^c\\\chi(i)\in S\setminus\{\chi(j)\}}}
P_a(S\setminus\{\chi(j)\},i)\omega_{ij}.
\end{equation}
Deleting the last vertex~$j$ of a path removes its color and the edge from its penultimate vertex~$i$. Conversely, appending~$j$ to a path using~$S\setminus\{\chi(j)\}$ cannot repeat a vertex. This proves the recurrence by induction on~$|S|$. For each fixed starting vertex~$a\in A^+$, the values~$P_a(S,j)$ form a table indexed by color sets~$S\subseteq[2L]$ and endpoints~$j\in A^c$. This table has~$2^{2L}|A^c|\leq N2^{2L}$ entries.

The table~$P_a(S,j)$ only counts paths starting at~$a\in A^+$. To construct cycles contained entirely in~$A^c$, we also need paths whose starting vertex~$r$ lies in~$A^c$. We therefore introduce a second path table. For~$r,j\in A^c$, let~$P_r^\circ(S,j)$ sum the weights of paths from~$r$ to~$j$ contained in~$A^c$, with vertex colors exactly~$S$, each once. Thus~$S$ includes~$\chi(r)$. Initialize~$P_r^\circ(\{\chi(r)\},r)=1$, corresponding to the path with no edges. All other singleton entries are zero. For~$|S|\geq2$, $\chi(r)\in S$, and~$\chi(j)\in S\setminus\{\chi(r)\}$, compute
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sk-cycle-recurrence}
P_r^\circ(S,j)=\sum_{\substack{i\in A^c\\\chi(i)\in S\setminus\{\chi(j)\}}}
P_r^\circ(S\setminus\{\chi(j)\},i)\omega_{ij}.
\end{equation}
All other entries are zero. Deleting or appending the last vertex gives the same induction as for~\eqref{eq:sk-path-recurrence}. Both tables are computed in increasing order of~$|S|$.

Both tables sum weights of paths ending in~$A^c$. Lemma~\ref{lem:sk-decomposition} requires path pieces to have two distinct endpoints in~$A^+$. Its cycle pieces meet~$A^+$ in at most one vertex. To obtain a path piece from a path counted by~$P_a(S,j)$, we append the edge~$\{j,a'\}$ with~$a'\in A^+\setminus\{a\}$. To obtain a cycle, we add~$\{j,a\}$ to a path counted by~$P_a(S,j)$, or~$\{j,r\}$ to a path counted by~$P_r^\circ(S,j)$. The size restrictions below ensure that each resulting cycle has at least three edges. For~$S\subseteq[2L]$, define the following sums.
\begin{itemize}
\item For~$a<a'$ in~$A^+$, let~$p_{aa'}(S)$ be the total weight of colorful paths between~$a$ and~$a'$ with nonempty interior in~$A^c$ and interior color set~$S$.
\item For~$a\in A^+$, let~$q_a(S)$ be the total weight of colorful cycles meeting~$A^+$ only at~$a$ and having outside color set~$S$.
\item Let~$\zeta(S)$ be the total weight of colorful cycles contained in~$A^c$ and having outside color set~$S$.
\end{itemize}
Multiplying by the added edge weights, summing the table entries, and correcting for repeated counts gives
\begin{align}
p_{aa'}(S)&=\sum_{j\in A^c}P_a(S,j)\omega_{ja'},&&a<a'\text{ in }A^+,\ |S|\geq1,\label{eq:sk-path-close}\\
q_a(S)&=\frac12\sum_{j\in A^c}P_a(S,j)\omega_{ja},&&a\in A^+,\ |S|\geq2,\label{eq:sk-return-close}\\
\zeta(S)&=\frac1{2|S|}\sum_{\substack{r\in A^c\\\chi(r)\in S}}\sum_{j\in A^c}P_r^\circ(S,j)\omega_{jr},&&|S|\geq3.\label{eq:sk-cycle-close}
\end{align}
Set these sums to zero outside the stated size ranges. Starting at the smaller endpoint counts each undirected path once. A cycle through~$a$ is counted in two orientations, while a cycle contained in~$A^c$ is counted in two orientations from each of its~$|S|$ roots. The denominators remove these multiplicities. The size restrictions exclude traversing a single edge twice.

\paragraph{Combining the pieces.}
The preceding part gives weight sums for individual path and cycle pieces, whereas our target~$f_A^\chi(k,S)$ sums weights of whole graphs. The second part of Lemma~\ref{lem:sk-decomposition} tells us which collections of pieces form the augmented graphs we need. For each such collection, the product of its piece weights is the weight of the corresponding original graph.

For example, if~$a\in A$ and~$S\cap T=\varnothing$, the product~$q_a(S)q_a(T)$ sums the weights of unions of two cycles whose only common vertex is~$a$, as in Figure~\ref{fig:sk-color-tables}(a). In general, products of piece sums may include collections in which pieces share vertices in~$A^c$ or whose union contains more than~$L$ original edges. Besides the restrictions on shared vertices and edge counts, we must check the degree conditions~\eqref{eq:sk-degree-conditions} and ensure that each graph is counted exactly once. We proceed in three steps.
\begin{enumerate}
\item We first combine the previously computed path and cycle weight sums, using disjoint color sets to prevent shared vertices in~$A^c$. We also exclude collections with more than~$L$ original edges.
\item The same collection can be formed by adding its pieces in different orders, so we correct for repeated counting. We then choose which direct edges within~$A^+$ to include, selecting each at most once.
\item Finally, we retain only collections whose unions satisfy the degree conditions~\eqref{eq:sk-degree-conditions}. We check degrees after all pieces and direct edges have been chosen, since further additions can change them.
\end{enumerate}

For the formal construction, we group collections by their number of original edges, the colors used in~$A^c$, and their degrees at~$A^+$. Each table entry stores the sum of the weights of the collections in one group. To check the degree conditions in step~3, we need not record every exact degree. At a vertex of~$A$, keep degrees~$0,1,2,3$ separately. Once the degree is at least four, further additions cannot lower it, so only parity matters. At~$0$, we use the same six states and retain states~$0,2,4$ in step~3. For a union~$H$ of selected pieces and direct edges, define its \emph{degree state} at~$a\in A^+$ by
\begin{equation*}
d_a=\degstate(\deg_H(a)),\qquad
\degstate(d)=\begin{cases}
d,&0\leq d\leq3,\\
4,&d\geq4\text{ even},\\
5,&d\geq5\text{ odd}.
\end{cases}
\end{equation*}
Here~$d$ is a nonnegative integer. The labels~$4$ and~$5$ represent even degrees at least four and odd degrees at least five, respectively. Degrees at each vertex add when the collections have disjoint edge sets. We can compute the new degree state from the two stored states because, for all nonnegative integers~$d,d'$,
\begin{equation*}
\degstate\bigl(\degstate(d)+\degstate(d')\bigr)=\degstate(d+d').
\end{equation*}
If~$d,d'<4$, the inner states equal~$d,d'$. Otherwise, $d+d'$ and~$\degstate(d)+\degstate(d')$ are both at least four and have the same parity.

We represent the table by a polynomial in formal variables~$t$, $x_i$ for~$i\in[2L]$, and~$y_a$ for~$a\in A^+$. Write~$x_S=\prod_{i\in S}x_i$. The coefficient of~$t^kx_S\prod_{a\in A^+}y_a^{d_a}$ is the sum of the weights of collections with~$k$ original edges, color set~$S$ on their vertices in~$A^c$, and degree state~$d_a$ at each~$a\in A^+$.

Polynomial multiplication multiplies the weight sums and adds the exponents. To exclude combinations with too many original edges or repeated colors, and to reduce the degree exponents to their stored states, we impose the relations
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sk-array-relations}
t^{L+1}=0,\qquad x_i^2=0\ (i\in[2L]),\qquad y_a^6=y_a^4\ (a\in A^+).
\end{equation}
The relation~$t^{L+1}=0$ discards terms with more than~$L$ original edges, and~$x_i^2=0$ discards terms with a repeated color~$i$. The relation~$y_a^6=y_a^4$ reduces each degree exponent~$d$ to~$\degstate(d)$. The array indices are~$0\leq k\leq L$, $S\subseteq[2L]$, and~$d_a\in\{0,\ldots,5\}$. For~$b=|A|$, each array has
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sk-array-size}
K_{b,L}=(L+1)2^{2L}6^{b+1}
\end{equation}
entries. The constant polynomial~$1$ represents the empty collection.


% BEGIN ADDED FIGURE 3
Figure~\ref{fig:sk-color-tables} illustrates the rule~$x_i^2=0$. Two pieces cannot be combined if their vertices in~$A^c$ use a common color, even when they share no vertex in~$A^c$. This restriction is required because~$f_A^\chi(k,S)$ includes only colorful graphs.
\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\manuscriptFigure{main-12-2-2-figures/sk-color-tables.pdf}{\manuscriptColorFigure}
\caption{The color test when combining two cycle pieces. Both panels show the same graph, with $A=\{a\}$ and $L\geq6$. Vertex names are outside the circles, and the numbers inside are their colors. The isolated auxiliary vertex $0$ is omitted. In (a), all four outside colors are distinct, so the two cycles can be combined. In (b), the distinct vertices $v$ and $w$ both have color $2$. Each cycle is colorful by itself, but their product contains $x_2^2$ and is discarded. The graph itself is valid. Section~\ref{subsec:sk-estimator} corrects this coloring restriction by reweighting and averaging.}
\label{fig:sk-color-tables}
\end{figure}
% END ADDED FIGURE 3

Using the previously computed sums~$p_{aa'}(S)$, $q_a(S)$, and~$\zeta(S)$, define~$\Psi_A$ by summing the following contributions over the indicated color sets~$S\subseteq[2L]$ and endpoints. This polynomial records the weight sums for individual pieces containing at least one vertex in~$A^c$.
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sk-piece-polynomial}
\begin{array}{lll}
\text{Piece} & \text{Contribution to }\Psi_A & \text{Condition}\\ \hline
\text{Cycle in }A^c & \zeta(S)t^{|S|}x_S & |S|\geq3\\
\text{Path, }a<a'\text{ in }A & p_{aa'}(S)t^{|S|+1}x_Sy_ay_{a'} & |S|\geq1\\
\text{Cycle at }a\in A & q_a(S)t^{|S|+1}x_Sy_a^2 & |S|\geq2\\
\text{Path from }0\text{ to }a\in A & p_{0a}(S)t^{|S|}x_Sy_0y_a & |S|\geq1\\
\text{Cycle at }0 & q_0(S)t^{|S|-1}x_Sy_0^2 & |S|\geq2
\end{array}
\end{equation}
Only original edges count toward the power of~$t$. In particular, a cycle through~$0$ has two auxiliary edges and hence~$|S|-1$ original edges.

Expanding~$\Psi_A^j$ gives ordered choices of~$j$ pieces. The relations~$x_i^2=0$ require their color sets to be disjoint. Since each piece uses a nonempty color set, the selected pieces are distinct, and each collection appears in~$j!$ orders. Dividing by~$j!$ counts it once. Each piece contains an original edge, so at most~$L$ pieces can contribute. Summing~$\Psi_A^j/j!$ over~$0\leq j\leq L$ gives~$E_A$. We form~$F_A$ by multiplying~$E_A$ by one factor for each direct edge. These edges have uncolored endpoints, so separate factors prevent repeated selection that the color restriction would not exclude.
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sk-assembled-polynomial}
\begin{aligned}
E_A=\sum_{j=0}^{L}\frac{\Psi_A^j}{j!}, \quad 
F_A=\prod_{a<a'\in A}(1+t\omega_{aa'}y_ay_{a'})
\prod_{a\in A}(1+\omega_{0a}y_0y_a)E_A.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Compute~$\Psi_A^j/j!$ successively through~$Y_0=1$ and~$Y_j=Y_{j-1}\Psi_A/j$ for~$1\leq j\leq L$, then sum~$E_A=\sum_{j=0}^L Y_j$.

The degree selection in step~3 requires state~$4$ at every~$a\in A$ and one of~$0,2,4$ at~$0$. We therefore claim that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sk-accepting-coefficient}
f_A^\chi(k,S)=\sum_{d\in\{0,2,4\}}\left[t^kx_Sy_0^d\prod_{a\in A}y_a^4\right]F_A,
\end{equation}
where brackets denote coefficients after reduction by~\eqref{eq:sk-array-relations}.

\begin{proof}[Proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:sk-colorful}]
 For a piece~$Q$, write
\begin{equation*}
m(Q)=|Q\cap\mathcal E_{N,2}|,\qquad
C_\chi(Q)=\{\chi(v):v\in V(Q)\cap A^c\}.
\end{equation*}
Let~$\mathcal Q\subseteq\mathscr P_A\cup\mathscr C_A$ consist of the colorful pieces~$Q$ that contain at least one vertex in~$A^c$ and satisfy~$m(Q)\leq L$. The path recurrences and formulas~\eqref{eq:sk-path-close}--\eqref{eq:sk-cycle-close} count each such piece once, so
\begin{equation*}
\Psi_A=\sum_{Q\in\mathcal Q}
\left(\prod_{e\in Q}\omega_e\right)
t^{m(Q)}x_{C_\chi(Q)}\prod_{a\in A^+}y_a^{\deg_Q(a)}.
\end{equation*}
This expansion verifies the coefficients computed from the path tables. The algorithm does not enumerate~$\mathcal Q$.

Expanding~$\Psi_A^j$ gives ordered choices of~$j$ pieces. The relations~$x_i^2=0$ and~$t^{L+1}=0$ discard choices with a repeated color on vertices in~$A^c$ or more than~$L$ original edges. The remaining pieces have nonempty, pairwise disjoint color sets and are therefore distinct. Each collection appears in exactly~$j!$ orders, so division by~$j!$ counts it once. Each piece has at least one original edge. Hence a collection with at most~$L$ original edges contains at most~$L$ pieces and occurs in~$E_A$.

The direct edge factors select a subset~$\mathcal D\subseteq\binom{A^+}{2}$. Together with a retained collection~$\mathcal C\subseteq\mathcal Q$, they give
\begin{equation*}
H=\mathcal D\cup\bigcup_{Q\in\mathcal C}Q.
\end{equation*}
Distinct pieces have disjoint vertex sets in~$A^c$, and each of their edges has at least one endpoint in~$A^c$. They therefore share no edges with one another or with~$\mathcal D$, so~$H$ is simple and degrees add. If~$|H\cap\mathcal E_{N,2}|\leq L$, its contribution is
\begin{equation*}
\left(\prod_{e\in H}\omega_e\right)
t^{|H\cap\mathcal E_{N,2}|}
x_{\chi(V(H)\cap A^c)}
\prod_{a\in A^+}y_a^{\degstate(\deg_H(a))}\,.
\end{equation*}
Otherwise it vanishes.

The coefficient selection in~\eqref{eq:sk-accepting-coefficient} requires even degree at~$0$ and even degree at least four at every~$a\in A$. Every used vertex in~$A^c$ has degree two, so~$H$ satisfies~\eqref{eq:sk-degree-conditions}. The second part of Lemma~\ref{lem:sk-decomposition} then gives
\begin{equation*}
\Gamma=H\cap\mathcal E_{N,2},\qquad H=\Gamma^+,\qquad
\Br(\Gamma)=A,\qquad \prod_{e\in H}\omega_e=W_h(\Gamma).
\end{equation*}
A vertex in~$A^c$ loses at most one edge when~$0$ is deleted, so~$V(H)\cap A^c=V(\Gamma)\setminus A$. Each contribution selected by~$t^kx_S$ therefore gives a graph~$\Gamma\in\mathcal G_A^\chi(k,S)$ with weight~$W_h(\Gamma)$. Conversely, each~$\Gamma\in\mathcal G_A^\chi(k,S)$ has the unique decomposition of Lemma~\ref{lem:sk-decomposition}. At most~$k\leq L$ pieces in this decomposition contain at least one vertex in~$A^c$. The contribution of~$\Gamma$ satisfies the color, edge count, and degree restrictions and is counted once. This proves~\eqref{eq:sk-accepting-coefficient}, including the empty graph when~$A=\varnothing$, $k=0$, and~$S=\varnothing$. The argument counts contributions before summing weights, so it also covers zero and negative weights.

For the operation bound, put~$b=|A|$ and~$\tilde N=N+2L+1$. Store pair weights in an array and color sets as~$2L$ binary flags. There are~$b+1$ tables~$P_a$ and~$N-b$ tables~$P_r^\circ$, hence~$N+1$ tables in total. Each has at most~$N4^L$ entries, and each recurrence has at most~$N$ summands. Computing all tables therefore requires evaluating at most~$(N+1)N^2 4^L$ summands. Including color tests, set generation, indexing, and the sums in~\eqref{eq:sk-path-close}--\eqref{eq:sk-cycle-close} costs~$O(\tilde N^6 4^L)$.

Each coefficient array multiplication examines at most
\(
K_{b,L}^2=36(L+1)^2\,16^L\,36^b
\)
pairs of entries, using~$O(\tilde N^2)$ operations per pair for color tests, degree reduction, and indexing. Computing~$E_A$ and multiplying the direct edge factors requires at most~$L+(b+1)^2$ such multiplications. Since~$L+1\leq\tilde N$ and~$L+(b+1)^2\leq2\tilde N^2$, the total cost is
\begin{equation*}
O\!\left((L+(b+1)^2)\tilde N^2 K_{b,L}^2\right)
=O\!\left(\tilde N^6\,16^L\,36^b\right).
\end{equation*}
Input preparation, initialization, scalar operations, and coefficient extraction fit the same bound, proving~\eqref{eq:sk-colorful-cost}.
\end{proof}


\subsection{Unbiased estimation and cost}\label{subsec:sk-estimator}

Proposition~\ref{prop:sk-colorful} computes the colorful part exactly. A plain average over random colorings would multiply each graph's weight by its probability of being colorful. We correct this bias by dividing each included contribution by its inclusion probability. We then average independent trials to reduce the error.

Fix integers~$0\leq D\leq N$ and~$L,R\geq1$, where~$R$ is the number of trials for each candidate branching set. We use~$\E_J$, $\E_\chi$, and~$\E_{J,\chi}$ for disorder, color, and joint expectations, respectively. For a fixed candidate~$A$, draw~$\chi:A^c\to[2L]$ with independent uniform colors. A fixed set of~$0\leq m\leq2L$ outside vertices receives distinct colors with probability
\begin{equation}\label{eq:color-inclusion}
\pi_{m,2L}=\frac{(2L)_m}{(2L)^m},\qquad
(2L)_m=(2L)(2L-1)\cdots(2L-m+1),\qquad \pi_{0,2L}=1.
\end{equation}
Every graph with at most~$L$ edges has at most~$2L$ outside vertices, so these probabilities are positive. Hold~$J$ fixed and consider a retained graph with branching set~$A$ and~$m$ outside vertices. Its coefficient is one when the graph is colorful and zero otherwise, with mean~$\pi_{m,2L}$. Dividing that coefficient by~$\pi_{m,2L}$ makes its mean one. This corrects each signed graph weight, without requiring it to be positive.

The correction is computable from the stored indices. Every graph in~$\mathcal G_A^\chi(k,S)$ has exactly~$|S|$ used vertices in~$A^c$, so all these graphs have the same inclusion probability~$\pi_{|S|,2L}$. We divide the whole entry by this probability, without identifying its individual graphs. Summing the corrected entries gives, for one trial and fixed~$J$,
\begin{equation*}
\E_\chi\!\left[\sum_{k=0}^{L}\sum_{S\subseteq[2L]}
\frac{f_A^\chi(k,S)}{\pi_{|S|,2L}}\right]
=\sum_{\substack{\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal E_N\\\Br(\Gamma)=A,\ |\Gamma|\leq L}}W_h(\Gamma).
\end{equation*}
The right side is the contribution of the candidate~$A$ to~$T^{\mathrm{SK}}_{N,D,L}$. For each~$A\subseteq[N]$ with~$|A|\leq D$ and each trial~$n\in\{1,\ldots,R\}$, let~$\chi_{A,n}:A^c\to[2L]$ assign independent uniform colors to the vertices. Take these colorings independently of one another and of~$J$. Define the \emph{randomized estimate} of~$T^{\mathrm{SK}}_{N,D,L}$ by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sk-estimator-definition}
\widetilde T^{\mathrm{SK}}_{N,D,L,R}
:=\sum_{\substack{A\subseteq[N]\\|A|\leq D}}
\frac1R\sum_{n=1}^{R}\sum_{k=0}^{L}\sum_{S\subseteq[2L]}
\frac{f_A^{\chi_{A,n}}(k,S)}{\pi_{|S|,2L}}\,.
\end{equation}
Thus we average~$R$ corrected color sums for each candidate~$A$, then add the results over all candidates. Algorithm~\ref{alg:sk-sum} computes this quantity.

\begin{algorithm}[!htbp]
\caption{\textsc{SKSum}$(N,h,\omega,D,L,R)$}\label{alg:sk-sum}
\begin{algorithmic}[1]
\Require Cached pair weights~$(\omega_{ij})$, external field~$h$, integers~$0\leq D\leq N$ and~$L,R\geq1$.
\State Compute~$\hh\gets\tanh h$, set~$\omega_{0i}=\omega_{i0}\gets\hh$ for~$i\in[N]$, compute~$\pi_{m,2L}$ for~$0\leq m\leq2L$, and initialize~$T\gets0$.
\For{each $A\subseteq[N]$ with $|A|\leq D$, generated by cardinality}
  \State $A^+\gets A\cup\{0\}$ and~$T_A\gets0$.
  \For{$n=1,\ldots,R$}
    \State Draw~$\chi:A^c\to[2L]$ with independent uniform colors, independently of~$J$ and all previous colorings.
    \State Initialize the path tables and coefficient arrays to zero for this trial.
    \State Compute~$P_a,P_r^\circ$ in increasing~$|S|$, using their initial states and~\eqref{eq:sk-path-recurrence}--\eqref{eq:sk-cycle-recurrence}.
    \State Compute~$p_{aa'},q_a,\zeta$ by~\eqref{eq:sk-path-close}--\eqref{eq:sk-cycle-close}, then form~$\Psi_A$ by~\eqref{eq:sk-piece-polynomial}.
    \State $Y\gets1$ and~$E\gets1$.
    \For{$j=1,\ldots,L$}
      \State $Y\gets Y\Psi_A/j$ and then~$E\gets E+Y$, using~\eqref{eq:sk-array-relations}.
    \EndFor
    \State Form~$F_A$ from~$E$ using the factors for direct edges in~\eqref{eq:sk-assembled-polynomial}.
    \State Extract~$f_A^\chi(k,S)$ by~\eqref{eq:sk-accepting-coefficient} and add~$\sum_{k=0}^L\sum_{S\subseteq[2L]}\pi_{|S|,2L}^{-1}f_A^\chi(k,S)$ to~$T_A$.
  \EndFor
  \State $T\gets T+T_A/R$.
\EndFor
\State \Return $T$.
\end{algorithmic}
\end{algorithm}

\begin{proposition}[SK evaluation]\label{prop:sk-estimator}
For integers~$0\leq D\leq N$ and~$L,R\geq1$, the estimate defined in~\eqref{eq:sk-estimator-definition} and computed by Algorithm~\ref{alg:sk-sum} satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sk-unbiased}
\E_\chi[\widetilde T^{\mathrm{SK}}_{N,D,L,R}\mid J]=T^{\mathrm{SK}}_{N,D,L}
\end{equation}
for every fixed realization of~$J$. Under~\eqref{eq:disorder},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sk-evaluation-error}
\E_{J,\chi}|\widetilde T^{\mathrm{SK}}_{N,D,L,R}-T^{\mathrm{SK}}_{N,D,L}|^2\leq\frac{C_+e^{2L}}{R}.
\end{equation}
For an absolute constant~$C_{\mathrm{alg}}$, the operation count on every finite input and every color outcome is at most
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sk-evaluation-cost}
C_{\mathrm{alg}}R(N+2L+1)^6 16^L\sum_{b=0}^{D}\binom Nb36^b.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The probability correction gives unbiasedness for fixed~$J$, but rare inclusions can have large corrected coefficients. We first bound the variance of each coefficient after~$R$ trials. We then use disorder orthogonality to control the error of the complete signed sum.
Fix a retained graph~$\Gamma$ and let~$m=|V(\Gamma)\setminus\Br(\Gamma)|$. Let~$\ind_n(\Gamma)$ indicate that its outside colors are distinct in trial~$n$ for~$A=\Br(\Gamma)$. This is the only candidate set at which~$\Gamma$ can appear, and its coefficient in the output is
\begin{equation*}
c_\Gamma=\frac1R\sum_{n=1}^R\frac{\ind_n(\Gamma)}{\pi_{m,2L}}.
\end{equation*}
The indicators are independent across trials and each has mean~$\pi_{m,2L}$. The bound~$n!\geq(n/e)^n$ gives~$\pi_{m,2L}\geq\pi_{2L,2L}=(2L)!/(2L)^{2L}\geq e^{-2L}$. Therefore
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sk-coefficient-variance}
\E_\chi c_\Gamma=1,\qquad
\E_\chi(c_\Gamma-1)^2=\frac{1-\pi_{m,2L}}{R\pi_{m,2L}}\leq\frac{e^{2L}}{R}.
\end{equation}
Summing the first identity against the fixed graph weights proves~\eqref{eq:sk-unbiased}. To estimate the error, first write the difference of the two graph sums as
\begin{equation*}
\widetilde T^{\mathrm{SK}}_{N,D,L,R}-T^{\mathrm{SK}}_{N,D,L}
=\sum_{\substack{\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal E_N\\|\Br(\Gamma)|\leq D,\ |\Gamma|\leq L}}
(c_\Gamma-1)W_h(\Gamma).
\end{equation*}
Graphs with the same candidate branching set are tested using the same random coloring, so their coefficients can be dependent. Their covariance terms need not vanish when~$J$ is held fixed. Instead, average also over~$J$. The full array~$(c_\Gamma-1)$ is independent of~$J$, and Lemma~\ref{lem:orthogonality} removes the cross terms between different graph weights. Applying it to this sum gives
\begin{align*}
\E_{J,\chi}|\widetilde T^{\mathrm{SK}}_{N,D,L,R}-T^{\mathrm{SK}}_{N,D,L}|^2
&=\sum_{\substack{|\Br(\Gamma)|\leq D\\|\Gamma|\leq L}}
\E_\chi(c_\Gamma-1)^2\E_J W_h(\Gamma)^2\\
&\leq\frac{e^{2L}}R\sum_{\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal E_N}\E_J W_h(\Gamma)^2
\leq\frac{C_+e^{2L}}R,
\end{align*}
where the last step uses~\eqref{eq:total-mass} and~$r_{N,2}\leq\bar r_{N,2}$.

For the operation count, fix a candidate set~$A$ of size~$b$ and one trial. Proposition~\ref{prop:sk-colorful} bounds the computation for a fixed coloring by~$O((N+2L+1)^6 16^L36^b)$. Computing inclusion probabilities, drawing colors, and accumulating the corrected entries fit the same bound.

The candidate sets can be generated in~$O((N+1)^2\sum_{b\leq D}\binom Nb)$ operations as in Proposition~\ref{prop:pure-evaluation}. Sum the bound for one trial over these sets and the~$R$ trials to obtain~\eqref{eq:sk-evaluation-cost}. The term~$b=0$ covers input preparation.
\end{proof}

To obtain the total approximation error, separate the error in evaluating the retained sum from the error of truncating the expansion. Given~$J$, the first has color mean zero by~\eqref{eq:sk-unbiased}, while the second is fixed. The cross term therefore vanishes, giving
\begin{align*}
\E_{J,\chi}|\widetilde T^{\mathrm{SK}}_{N,D,L,R}-\widehat Z_N|^2
=\E_{J,\chi}|\widetilde T^{\mathrm{SK}}_{N,D,L,R}-T^{\mathrm{SK}}_{N,D,L}|^2
+\E_J|T^{\mathrm{SK}}_{N,D,L}-\widehat Z_N|^2.
\end{align*}

Substituting Propositions~\ref{prop:sk-truncation} and~\ref{prop:sk-estimator} gives, for~$D<\lfloor\gamma N\rfloor$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sk-complete-error}
\E|\widetilde T^{\mathrm{SK}}_{N,D,L,R}-\widehat Z_N|^2
\leq C_+\theta^{2(L+1)}+2C_{\mathrm{SK}}e^{-\Phi_N(D+1)}+C_+\theta^{3\gamma N}+\frac{C_+e^{2L}}R.
\end{equation}
The first three terms are truncation errors. The last is the error from random coloring. Increasing~$L$ reduces the edge tail but requires more trials to control the last term. Thus~$D$ controls branching, $L$ controls the number of edges, and~$R$ controls evaluation error. Section~\ref{subsec:cutoff-selection} chooses them together so that both the total error and the operation count meet the requested bounds.

\section{Proof of the main results}\label{sec:error-complexity}

This section, by specifying the relevant constants, turns the truncation estimates into mean square approximation for $\hat{Z}_N$, combining with  a lower tail bound for $\hat{Z}_N$, we prove Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-size}.

\subsection{Choosing the cutoffs}\label{subsec:cutoff-selection}

Given a requested mean-square error~$u\in(0,1)$, we use~\eqref{eq:higher-truncation} and~\eqref{eq:sk-complete-error} to choose parameters that make the corresponding error bound at most~$u$. We also need the evaluation cost to be polynomial in~$N$ and~$u^{-1}$. Let~$k$ denote the cutoff on used vertices for higher-order mixtures and on branching vertices for SK, where we take~$D=k$. The SK parameters~$L$ and~$R$ will be chosen after~$k$.

The dependence on~$k$ in these error bounds is through~$e^{-\Phi_N(k+1)}$. The index~$k+1$ is the first discarded size. Recall
\begin{equation}\label{eq:algorithm-support-scale}
\Phi_N(t)=\kappa t\log\frac{N}{C_\star t}\quad(t>0),
\qquad \Phi_N(0)=0,
\end{equation}
with the constants from Section~\ref{subsec:higher-support} for higher-order mixtures and Section~\ref{subsec:sk-support} for SK. For higher-order mixtures, $\kappa$ may depend on~$N,h$, but~$\kappa\geq\kappa_{\min}>0$. For SK, $\kappa=\kappa_{\min}=1$.

It suffices to bound~$e^{-\Phi_N(k+1)}$, the powers of~$\theta$ in~\eqref{eq:higher-truncation} and~\eqref{eq:sk-complete-error}, and, for SK, $e^{2L}/R$ by~$u/C_\Lambda$. With
\begin{equation}\label{eq:algorithm-cutoff-constants}
C_\Lambda=\begin{cases}
8(2+C_+),&p_m\geq3,\\
8(2C_{\mathrm{SK}}+3C_+),&\text{SK},
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
the two mean-square bounds are then at most~$(2+C_+)u/C_\Lambda=u/8$ and~$(2C_{\mathrm{SK}}+3C_+)u/C_\Lambda=u/8$, respectively. In particular, we need to choose~$k$ so that~$e^{-\Phi_N(k+1)}\leq u/C_\Lambda$.

The truncation estimates apply for~$k<\lfloor\gamma N\rfloor$, with~$\gamma$ chosen small enough that~$e^{-\Phi_N(k+1)}$ decreases as~$k$ increases. We choose a second fixed constant~$\nu>0$ so that, for sufficiently large~$N$, the condition~$u>C_\Lambda e^{-\nu N}$ guarantees that some integer~$0\leq k<\lfloor\gamma N\rfloor$ satisfies~$e^{-\Phi_N(k+1)}\leq u/C_\Lambda$. It also ensures~$\theta^{2\gamma N/P}\leq u/C_\Lambda$ for higher-order mixtures and~$\theta^{3\gamma N}\leq u/C_\Lambda$ for SK. These terms depend only on~$N$ and cannot be reduced by increasing~$k$. The following lemma gives the required cutoff and a polynomial bound on its enumeration cost.

\begin{lemma}[Choosing the vertex cutoff]\label{lem:cutoff-selection}
There exist constants~$\gamma\in(0,1/4)$, $\nu>0$, and an integer~$N_0\geq P$, depending only on the fixed model and~$\beta_0,\rho_0$, with the following property. For every~$N\geq N_0$ and~$C_\Lambda e^{-\nu N}<u<1$, one can choose an integer~$0\leq k<\lfloor\gamma N\rfloor$ satisfying the truncation requirement~$e^{-\Phi_N(k+1)}\leq u/C_\Lambda$ and the enumeration bound
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cutoff-cost}
\sum_{j=0}^{k}\binom Nj t^j\leq(N+1)\left(\frac{C_\Lambda}{u}\right)^{C_t}
\end{equation}
for every fixed~$t\geq1$. Here~$C_t>0$ depends only on the fixed model, $\beta_0,\rho_0$, and~$t$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Choose
\begin{equation*}
\gamma=\min\left\{\frac18,\frac{e^{-(1+2/\kappa_{\min})}}{C_\star}\right\}
\end{equation*}
and~$N_0=\lceil\max\{P,4,2/\gamma\}\rceil$. For~$0<x\leq\gamma N$, we have~$\Phi_N'(x)=\kappa(\log(N/(C_\star x))-1)\geq\kappa_{\min}(\log(1/(C_\star\gamma))-1)\geq2$. Thus~$\Phi_N$ is increasing on~$(0,\gamma N]$. For~$N\geq N_0$, the inequality~$\lfloor\gamma N\rfloor\geq\gamma N/2$ gives
\begin{equation*}
\Phi_N(\lfloor\gamma N\rfloor)
\geq\Phi_N(\gamma N/2)
\geq\frac{\kappa_{\min}\gamma N}{2}\log\frac{2}{C_\star\gamma}.
\end{equation*}

The terms~$C_+\theta^{2\gamma N/P}$ and~$C_+\theta^{3\gamma N}$ in~\eqref{eq:higher-truncation} and~\eqref{eq:sk-complete-error}, respectively, equal~$C_+e^{-c_1N}$, where
\begin{equation*}
c_1=\begin{cases}
(\gamma/P)\log(1/\theta^2),&p_m\geq3,\\
(3\gamma/2)\log(1/\theta^2),&\text{SK}.
\end{cases}
\end{equation*}
Set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:algorithm-remainder-scale}
\nu=\frac14\min\left\{1,\ c_1,\ \frac{\kappa_{\min}\gamma}{2}\log\frac{2}{C_\star\gamma}\right\}.
\end{equation}
Let~$N\geq N_0$ and~$C_\Lambda e^{-\nu N}<u<1$. With~$\Lambda=\log(C_\Lambda/u)$, we have~$0<\Lambda<\nu N$ and~$\Phi_N(\lfloor\gamma N\rfloor)\geq4\nu N>\Lambda$. Consequently, there is an integer~$0\leq k<\lfloor\gamma N\rfloor$ satisfying~$\Phi_N(k+1)\geq\Lambda$. Choose the least such integer. Then~$e^{-\Phi_N(k+1)}\leq e^{-\Lambda}=u/C_\Lambda$. Minimality gives~$\Phi_N(k)<\Lambda$ for~$k\geq1$. The same inequality holds for~$k=0$, since~$\Phi_N(0)=0$. The choice of~$\nu$ also gives~$c_1N\geq4\nu N>4\Lambda$, so
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cutoff-error}
e^{-c_1N}\leq e^{-4\Lambda}.
\end{equation}

Fix~$t\geq1$. If~$k=0$, the sum in~\eqref{eq:cutoff-cost} equals one, so the claimed bound holds. Suppose~$k\geq1$. The bound~$\binom Nj\leq(eN/j)^j$ for~$j\geq1$ and the monotonicity of~$x\log(etN/x)$ on~$(0,N]$ give
\begin{equation*}
\sum_{j=0}^{k}\binom Nj t^j\leq(k+1)\exp\left(k\log\frac{etN}{k}\right).
\end{equation*}
Since~$k<\gamma N$ and~$\kappa\geq\kappa_{\min}$, we have~$\Phi_N(k)\geq\kappa_{\min}k\log(1/(C_\star\gamma))>0$. Define~$C_t=\kappa_{\min}^{-1}\bigl(1+\log(etC_\star)/\log(1/(C_\star\gamma))\bigr)$. Using this lower bound on~$\Phi_N(k)$ and~$C_\star\geq1$, we obtain
\begin{equation*}
k\log\frac{etN}{k}
=\frac{\Phi_N(k)}{\kappa}+k\log(etC_\star)
\leq C_t\Phi_N(k)<C_t\Lambda.
\end{equation*}
The enumeration sum is therefore at most~$(N+1)e^{C_t\Lambda}=(N+1)(C_\Lambda/u)^{C_t}$, proving~\eqref{eq:cutoff-cost}. The cutoff can be found by testing at most~$\lfloor\gamma N\rfloor$ successive integers, using~$O(N)$ operations.
\end{proof}

Fix the constants~$\gamma,\nu,N_0$ constructed in the proof, and write~$\Lambda=\log(C_\Lambda/u)$ for the rest of this subsection. For higher-order mixtures, we evaluate the retained sum exactly. For SK, we must also control the edge error~$C_+\theta^{2(L+1)}$ and the coloring error~$C_+e^{2L}/R$. Increasing~$L$ decreases the first term but increases the second unless~$R$ also increases. We choose~$L$ and~$R$ so that both terms are at most~$C_+e^{-\Lambda}$.

The algorithm uses these truncations when~$N\geq N_0$ and~$\Lambda<\nu N$. Otherwise it evaluates the spin sum exactly.

\begin{algorithm}[!htbp]
\caption{\textsc{GraphApprox}$(N,\beta,h,J,u)$}\label{alg:graph-approx}
\begin{algorithmic}[1]
\Require Finite disorder array~$J$, $N\geq P$, parameters satisfying~\eqref{eq:field-range}, and~$u\in(0,1)$.
\State Compute~$\omega_e=\tanh x_e$ and set~$\Lambda\gets\log(C_\Lambda/u)$.
\If{$N<N_0$ or $\Lambda\geq\nu N$}
  \State \Return the exact spin average~$G([N])$ in~\eqref{eq:local-spin-average}.
\EndIf
\State Compute~$\kappa$ by~\eqref{eq:field-dependent-kappa} for higher-order mixtures, or set~$\kappa\gets1$ for SK.
\State Find the smallest~$0\leq k<\lfloor\gamma N\rfloor$ satisfying~$\Phi_N(k+1)\geq\Lambda$.
\If{$p_m\geq3$}
  \State \Return $\textsc{SupportSum}(N,h,\omega,k)$.
\Else
  \State $D\gets k$, $L\gets\lceil\Lambda/\log(1/\theta^2)\rceil$, and~$R\gets\lceil e^{2L+\Lambda}\rceil$.
  \State \Return $\textsc{SKSum}(N,h,\omega,D,L,R)$.
\EndIf
\end{algorithmic}
\end{algorithm}

\begin{proposition}[Mean-square approximation]\label{prop:mean-square}
Suppose the disorder variables are independent and have a common symmetric law with unit variance, as in~\eqref{eq:disorder}. For every~$N\geq P$, specified~$\beta\in[0,\beta_0]$, external field~$|h|\leq\rho_0N^{-1/4}$, and~$u\in(0,1)$, Algorithm~\ref{alg:graph-approx} returns~$T$ satisfying
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mean-square-guarantee}
\E|T-\widehat Z_N|^2\leq u.
\end{equation}
There is a constant~$C>0$, depending only on the fixed model and~$\beta_0,\rho_0$, such that the algorithm uses at most~$CN^Cu^{-C}$ operations on every finite input and every random outcome. It is deterministic for higher-order mixtures and randomized for SK.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Suppose first that~$N\geq N_0$ and~$\Lambda<\nu N$. The latter condition is equivalent to~$u>C_\Lambda e^{-\nu N}$. The algorithm uses the cutoff constructed in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:cutoff-selection}. The cutoffs depend only on~$N,h,u$ and the fixed parameters, so the earlier mean-square estimates apply with cutoffs independent of the disorder.

For higher-order mixtures, Proposition~\ref{prop:pure-evaluation} evaluates the retained sum exactly. Proposition~\ref{prop:higher-truncation}, Lemma~\ref{lem:cutoff-selection}, and~\eqref{eq:cutoff-error} give
\begin{align*}
\E|T_{N,k}-\widehat Z_N|^2
&\leq2e^{-\Lambda}+C_+e^{-4\Lambda}\\
&\leq(2+C_+)e^{-\Lambda}=\frac u8.
\end{align*}
The cost bound~\eqref{eq:pure-evaluation-cost} and Lemma~\ref{lem:cutoff-selection} with~$t=2$ give at most~$C_P(N+1)^{P+3}e^{C_2\Lambda}$ operations.

For SK, the choices of~$L$ and~$R$ ensure~$\theta^{2(L+1)}\leq e^{-\Lambda}$ and~$e^{2L}/R\leq e^{-\Lambda}$. The combined error estimate~\eqref{eq:sk-complete-error}, Lemma~\ref{lem:cutoff-selection}, and~\eqref{eq:cutoff-error} therefore give
\begin{align*}
\E\left|\widetilde T^{\mathrm{SK}}_{N,k,L,R}-\widehat Z_N\right|^2
&\leq(2C_{\mathrm{SK}}+2C_+)e^{-\Lambda}+C_+e^{-4\Lambda}\\
&\leq(2C_{\mathrm{SK}}+3C_+)e^{-\Lambda}=\frac u8.
\end{align*}
By~\eqref{eq:sk-evaluation-cost} and Lemma~\ref{lem:cutoff-selection} with~$t=36$, the cost is at most
\begin{equation*}
C_{\mathrm{alg}}R(N+2L+1)^6\,16^L(N+1)e^{C_{36}\Lambda}.
\end{equation*}
Since~$L\leq1+\Lambda/\log(1/\theta^2)$ and~$\Lambda<\nu N$, we have~$N+2L+1\leq C(N+1)$. Also, $R\leq2e^{2L+\Lambda}$, so
\begin{align*}
R16^Le^{C_{36}\Lambda}
\leq2\exp\left((2+\log16)L+(1+C_{36})\Lambda\right)
\leq Ce^{C'\Lambda}
\end{align*}
for fixed constants~$C,C'>0$. The SK cost is consequently at most~$C(N+1)^7e^{C'\Lambda}$. In both models, the identity~$e^\Lambda=C_\Lambda/u$ converts these estimates into polynomial bounds in~$N$ and~$u^{-1}$.

It remains to consider exact enumeration. Here~$G([N])=\widehat Z_N$, so the error is zero. Its cost is at most~$C_P(N+1)^{P+1}2^N$. If~$N<N_0$, then~$2^N$ is bounded by a fixed constant. Otherwise~$\Lambda\geq\nu N$, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:exact-enumeration-cost}
2^N\leq e^{(\log2/\nu)\Lambda}
=\left(\frac{C_\Lambda}{u}\right)^{\log2/\nu}.
\end{equation}
Thus exact enumeration also satisfies the claimed polynomial bound.

Computing the input weights and searching for~$k$ add only polynomial work in~$N$. Computing the SK integer ceilings by comparisons and increments costs~$O(L+R)$, which is covered by the preceding estimates. Increasing~$C$ covers all cases.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Lower tail bound for $\hat{Z}_N$}\label{subsec:lower-tail}

To obtain logarithmic accuracy, we need to control the probability that~$\widehat Z_N$ is close to zero. On a suitable event determined by the coupling magnitudes, we will express~$\widehat Z_N$ as a noise average of a positive function of the independent coupling signs. The following consequence of reverse hypercontractivity reduces the required lower-tail bound to a second-moment estimate.

Let~$\Omega=\{-1,1\}^m$ with~$m\geq1$, and let~$\E$ denote uniform expectation on~$\Omega$ in the definition and lemma below. For~$f:\Omega\to\mathbb R$ and~$0<\vartheta<1$, define
\begin{equation*}
(\mathsf N_\vartheta f)(\epsilon)
=2^{-m}\sum_{\eta\in\Omega}f(\eta)
\prod_{i=1}^m(1+\vartheta\epsilon_i\eta_i),
\qquad \epsilon\in\Omega.
\end{equation*}
Equivalently, each coordinate independently retains its sign with probability~$\vartheta$ and otherwise receives a uniform sign. For~$B\subseteq[m]$, the monomial~$w_B(\epsilon)=\prod_{i\in B}\epsilon_i$ satisfies
\begin{equation*}
\mathsf N_\vartheta w_B=\vartheta^{|B|}w_B.
\end{equation*}

\begin{lemma}[Negative moment bound]\label{lem:reverse-hypercontractivity}
Let~$0<\vartheta<1$ and~$s=\vartheta^{-1/2}-1$. Every function~$f:\Omega\to(0,\infty)$ with~$\E f=1$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:reverse-hypercontractivity}
\E\bigl[(\mathsf N_\vartheta f)^{-s}\bigr]\leq\E f^2.
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Set~$q=1-\sqrt\vartheta$, so~$s=q/(1-q)$ and~$\vartheta=(1-q)/(1+s)$. The operator~$\mathsf N_\vartheta$ is the product of the uniform coordinate-resampling operators at time~$t=\log(1/\vartheta)$. Thus~\cite{MOS13}*{Corollary~1.11}, with input exponent~$q$ and output exponent~$-s$, gives
\begin{equation*}
\E\bigl[(\mathsf N_\vartheta f)^{-s}\bigr]
\leq(\E f^q)^{-s/q}.
\end{equation*}
H\"older's inequality gives
\begin{equation*}
1=\E f
=\E\!\left[(f^q)^{1/(2-q)}(f^2)^{(1-q)/(2-q)}\right]
\leq(\E f^q)^{1/(2-q)}(\E f^2)^{(1-q)/(2-q)}.
\end{equation*}
Hence~$\E f^q\geq(\E f^2)^{-(1-q)}$, and
\begin{equation*}
\E\bigl[(\mathsf N_\vartheta f)^{-s}\bigr]
\leq(\E f^2)^{s(1-q)/q}=\E f^2.
\end{equation*}
\end{proof}

For the partition function, the cube has one coordinate per interaction, so~$m=|\mathcal E_N|$. We will use~$\vartheta=\theta=\beta_0/\beta_+\in(0,1)$ from~\eqref{eq:algorithm-temperature-constants}. Define the exponent~$s$ in Algorithm~\ref{alg:log-approx} and the tail constant~$c$ in Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-size} by
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lower-tail-constants}
s=\theta^{-1/2}-1>0,\qquad c=\frac{\operatorname{arctanh}(\theta/2)}{\beta_0}.
\end{equation}
The event
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bounded-coupling-event}
\mathcal B_N=\bigcap_{p\in\mathcal P}
\left\{\max_{e\in\mathcal E_{N,p}}|a_pJ_e|\leq cN^{(p-1)/2}\right\}
\end{equation}
implies~$|x_e|\leq\beta_0c$ and hence~$|\omega_e|\leq\tanh(\beta_0c)=\theta/2$ for every edge. A union bound gives~$\pr(\mathcal B_N^c)\leq\tau_N$. This event will ensure positivity of the function to which we apply Lemma~\ref{lem:reverse-hypercontractivity}. It is used only in the proof, and the algorithm does not modify the disorder.

\begin{proposition}[Lower tail]\label{prop:lower-tail}
For every~$N\geq P$, specified~$\beta\in[0,\beta_0]$, and~$|h|\leq\rho_0N^{-1/4}$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lower-tail}
\E[\widehat Z_N^{-s}\ind_{\mathcal B_N}]\leq C_+,
\qquad \pr(\widehat Z_N<z)\leq C_+z^s+\tau_N\quad(z>0).
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
By symmetry, represent the disorder in law as~$J_e=\epsilon_e|J_e|$, where the~$\epsilon_e$ are independent uniform signs, independent of the magnitudes. This also covers an atom at zero by assigning an independent sign there. Then~$\omega_e=\epsilon_e|\omega_e|$. Fix the magnitudes and let~$\E_\epsilon$ denote uniform expectation on~$\{-1,1\}^{\mathcal E_N}$. The event~$\mathcal B_N$ depends only on the magnitudes, so restricting to this event does not change the conditional sign law.

To construct a function whose noise average is~$\widehat Z_N$, we divide each edge weight by~$\theta$ to cancel the factor~$\theta^{|\Gamma|}$ produced by the noise. Define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:inflated-sign-function}
f(\epsilon)=\E_h\prod_e\left(1+\frac{|\omega_e|}{\theta}\epsilon_e\sigma_e\right)
=\sum_{\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal E_N}\hh^{|\partial\Gamma|}
\prod_{e\in\Gamma}\frac{|\omega_e|\epsilon_e}{\theta}.
\end{equation}
Here~$\E_h$ averages the spins with~$\epsilon$ fixed. On~$\mathcal B_N$, every factor in the product is at least~$1/2$, so~$f>0$. Averaging over the signs leaves only the empty graph, giving~$\E_\epsilon f=1$. The monomial identity above yields
\begin{equation*}
(\mathsf N_\theta f)(\epsilon)
=\sum_{\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal E_N}\hh^{|\partial\Gamma|}
\prod_{e\in\Gamma}|\omega_e|\epsilon_e
=\widehat Z_N(\epsilon).
\end{equation*}
Lemma~\ref{lem:reverse-hypercontractivity} therefore gives, on~$\mathcal B_N$,
\begin{equation*}
\E_\epsilon\widehat Z_N^{-s}\leq\E_\epsilon f^2.
\end{equation*}
Orthogonality of the sign monomials gives, for every magnitude array,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:conditional-second-moment}
\E_\epsilon f^2=\sum_{\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal E_N}
\hh^{2|\partial\Gamma|}\prod_{e\in\Gamma}\frac{|\omega_e|^2}{\theta^2}.
\end{equation}
Averaging over the magnitudes, whose expectation we denote by~$\E_{|J|}$, and using~$\E_\epsilon f^2\geq0$, we obtain
\begin{align*}
\E[\widehat Z_N^{-s}\ind_{\mathcal B_N}]
&=\E_{|J|}\!\left[\ind_{\mathcal B_N}\E_\epsilon\widehat Z_N^{-s}\right]\\
&\leq\E_{|J|}[\ind_{\mathcal B_N}\E_\epsilon f^2]
\leq\E_{|J|}\E_\epsilon f^2\\
&=\sum_{\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal E_N}\hh^{2|\partial\Gamma|}
\prod_{e\in\Gamma}\frac{r_{N,|e|}}{\theta^2}
\leq C_+.
\end{align*}
For the last inequality, $r_{N,p}/\theta^2\leq\bar r_{N,p}$, and Proposition~\ref{prop:mass} bounds the sum with these comparison weights. Markov's inequality on~$\mathcal B_N$ and~$\pr(\mathcal B_N^c)\leq\tau_N$ give
\begin{equation*}
\pr(\widehat Z_N<z)
\leq\pr(\mathcal B_N^c)+z^s\E[\widehat Z_N^{-s}\ind_{\mathcal B_N}]
\leq\tau_N+C_+z^s.
\end{equation*}
\end{proof}

\subsection{Proof of the main theorem}\label{subsec:main-proofs}

It remains to combine the mean-square and lower-tail estimates with Algorithm~\ref{alg:log-approx}. The lower-tail bound supplies a positive level below which~$\widehat Z_N$ rarely falls. Above that level, an absolute error small compared with the level is also a small relative error, so taking the logarithm is safe. The two budgets in the algorithm separate these two ways the approximation can fail.

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-size}]
Let~$z,u$ be the budgets in Algorithm~\ref{alg:log-approx}, and let~$T$ be its output from~\textsc{GraphApprox}. The budgets satisfy~$0<z<1$ and~$0<u<1/8$, so Proposition~\ref{prop:mean-square} applies. We first identify an event on which~$T$ is positive and its logarithm has the required accuracy. On the event
\begin{equation*}
\widehat Z_N\geq z,\qquad |T-\widehat Z_N|\leq\varepsilon z/2,
\end{equation*}
we have~$T\geq z(1-\varepsilon/2)>0$ and~$|T/\widehat Z_N-1|\leq\varepsilon/2$. The bound~$|\log(1+x)|\leq2|x|$ for~$|x|\leq1/2$ gives~$|\log T-\log\widehat Z_N|\leq\varepsilon$. The other factors in~$Z_N$ are computed exactly, so the output has the same error for~$\log Z_N$.

We now bound the probability that this event fails. Proposition~\ref{prop:lower-tail} controls small values of~$\widehat Z_N$, and Markov's inequality controls the absolute error of~$T$. The budgets in Algorithm~\ref{alg:log-approx} satisfy~$C_+z^s=\delta/2$ and~$4u/(\varepsilon^2z^2)=\delta/2$. Consequently,
\begin{align*}
\pr(|\widehat L_N-\log Z_N|>\varepsilon)
&\leq\pr(\widehat Z_N<z)+\pr(|T-\widehat Z_N|>\varepsilon z/2)\\
&\leq\tau_N+C_+z^s+\frac{4\E|T-\widehat Z_N|^2}{\varepsilon^2z^2}
\leq\tau_N+\delta/2+\delta/2.
\end{align*}
No independence between~$T$ and~$\widehat Z_N$ is needed. The fallback when~$T\leq0$ occurs only outside the event above and is covered by this bound.

To finish, substitute~$u^{-1}=8(2C_+)^{2/s}\varepsilon^{-2}\delta^{-(1+2/s)}$ into the cost bound of Proposition~\ref{prop:mean-square}. This gives~$CN^C\varepsilon^{-C}\delta^{-C}$. Computing~$\log\overline Z_N$ and the other explicit terms adds only polynomial work in~$N$. All constants have the stated dependence.
\end{proof}
